## Alignment notes

These record where the PRD left a choice open, or where this plan reads the PRD in a specific way. An open note must be resolved, by confirming it or by amending the PRD, before the item that relies on it starts. New notes are added here as implementation raises them.

| # | Note | Status | Affects |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| N1 | The PRD named no JS package manager or workspace tool. | Decided: pnpm workspaces. Recorded in PRD 1.5 §10.9. | 1 |
| N2 | Only `schemaVersion` 1 exists in the MVP, but FR-DATA-03 AC1/AC3, FR-DATA-01 AC2/AC3, FR-SYNC-01 AC2, and FR-SYNC-07 AC1/AC2/AC4 need two versions. | Decided: a test-only synthetic version, numbered one above the newest real version and renumbered before a real version takes that number. Its fixtures live outside the published `/schema` tree, it is served only by test servers, and a build tag keeps it out of release binaries. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-DATA-03 and §6.4. | 77, 79, 84, 101 |
| N3 | Ownership transfer (FR-TEN-06) is v1, but FR-GDPR-02 (MVP) requires a sole owner to hand over before deleting their account. The PRD did not say who may grant `owner`. | Decided: only an `owner` may grant `owner`, or demote or remove an owner; admins manage roles up to `admin`. §5.6 has a separate row for this, and FR-TEN-03 AC4 tests it. The MVP handover is promoting another member, tested by FR-GDPR-02 AC4. Recorded in PRD 1.5. | 20, 111 |
| N4 | The authoring export (MVP) includes canvas annotations, which are created only by FR-EDIT-12 (v1). | Decided: the `annotations` section is reserved from the first format version and stays empty in the MVP. A server that cannot import a section's content refuses the archive and names the section (FR-DATA-01 AC12), so an MVP server never drops annotations from a v1 archive. FR-EDIT-12 AC3 adds export and import of annotations. Recorded in PRD 1.5. | 83, 84, 127 |
| N5 | Journey J4 step 3 cited FR-SNAP-05 (restore draft, v1) for the snapshot list and download link. | Fixed: PRD 1.5 cites FR-SNAP-02. | 81, 118 |
| N6 | §1.4 listed FR-API-01…06 (no FR-API-06 exists) and FR-GODOT-01…10 (omitting FR-GODOT-11). | Fixed: PRD 1.5 lists FR-API-01…05 and FR-GODOT-01…11. | — |
| N7 | FR-PUB-01 (MVP) lists `/changelog`, while FR-PUB-09 (v1) built it from release notes with a feed. | Decided: `/changelog` is generated from release notes in the MVP, tested by FR-PUB-01 AC5; FR-PUB-09 adds only the feed. NFR-SEC-17 announces security releases in the changelog, and in the feed from v1. FR-API-05 deprecations go in the release notes and do not need the feed. Recorded in PRD 1.5. | 108, 122, 131 |
| N8 | FR-ADMIN-04 AC2 (upgrade from the previous release) cannot run before a first release exists, but the traceability gate needs every MVP criterion to pass. | Decided: until the first release, the upgrade test seeds from the main branch's migrations (item 5); item 119 switches the seed to the previous release. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-ADMIN-04. | 5, 119 |
| N9 | FR-ENT-01 AC1 requires one enforcement site per limit, but `snapshot_retention` is enforced only by FR-SNAP-06 (SaaS). | Decided: a limit whose enforcing requirement is in a later phase is marked inert in the plan file and read by no code until then. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-ENT-01. | 21, 117 |
| N10 | Play mode (FR-PLAY-01 AC1) must run client-side on the published format, but the serializer is server-side. | Decided: item 71 adds a TypeScript projection of the draft into the published format, and item 89 cross-checks it against the Go serializer of item 76, so the serializer does not wait for the canvas (§12.6). No PRD change. | 71, 76, 89 |
| N11 | FR-API-01 AC3 (OpenAPI compared against the previous release) cannot run before a first release, the same problem as N8. | Decided: until the first release, the gate compares against the main branch, with a recorded per-PR override because no client depends on `v1` yet; item 119 switches the baseline. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-API-01. | 7, 119 |
| N12 | An owner in the 30-day account-deletion grace period still counted as an owner, so two owners could both delete their accounts and leave a workspace with none. FR-TEN-02 AC2 tested removal only. | Decided: a pending-deletion owner does not count, for the sole-owner check and the last-owner rule; FR-TEN-02 AC2 covers demotion. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-GDPR-02 and FR-TEN-02. | 19, 111 |
| N13 | With `STORYCREEP_SIGNUP=open`, a self-registered first user disabled first-run setup. | Decided: registration is refused with `setup_required` until setup completes, and the setup route closes when setup completes, not when a user exists. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-ADMIN-02 AC4 and FR-AUTH-01. | 13, 17 |
| N14 | FR-ENT-01 listed `nodes per project` and `API requests per minute` as limits, which ADR-010 does not, and the plan enforced an unlisted `boards` limit. | Decided: the plan file holds exactly the ADR-010 keys. Boards, nodes, and API requests are metered only; request rates are bounded by the operational rate limits. An inert limit is one no enforcement site checks; reading the plan file as data is allowed. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-ENT-01 and FR-ENT-02. | 21, 23, 30, 117 |
| N15 | FR-DATA-01 AC12 was defined against "a server without FR-EDIT-12", which no longer exists after item 127, and the MVP schema did not define annotation items. | Decided: the first archive schema defines the annotation item, and after item 127 the AC12 fixture uses a test-only section that no importer handles. Recorded in PRD 1.5 FR-DATA-01. | 83, 84, 127 |
| N16 | Migrations need a superuser, because they create the `BYPASSRLS` maintenance role, but the application must connect as a non-superuser (§5.4). The PRD did not say where each login's credentials come from. | Decided: two URLs. `STORYCREEP_DATABASE_URL` is the application's login and `STORYCREEP_DATABASE_ADMIN_URL` the superuser that applies migrations. Migrating creates or updates the application login from its URL, with the password the server logs in with, sending a SCRAM verifier rather than the password, and grants it `storycreep_app`. Because roles belong to the cluster, every Storycreep database on a server grants its privileges to the same `storycreep_app`, so migrating also revokes `CONNECT` on the database from `PUBLIC` and from `storycreep_app`, grants it to the application login, and to the admin login if the revocation took its `CONNECT`, and fails if `PUBLIC` or `storycreep_app` can still connect, which keeps the login of one Storycreep database out of another. A server that does not apply migrations itself refuses a migrated database that `PUBLIC` or `storycreep_app` can connect to again, as after a dump is restored into a new database. It then connects as that login and fails unless the database it reaches has the migrations. The admin URL must name the same database, and its login must not be, or act as, one of the server's roles, which the first migration makes unable to log in. The server refuses an application login that is a superuser, has `BYPASSRLS`, `CREATEROLE`, or `REPLICATION`, or owns the database or objects in it, directly or through a role, or that is a member of `storycreep_definer`, `storycreep_maintenance`, or one of the predefined roles that give access to the database server's files and programs (`pg_execute_server_program`, `pg_read_server_files`, `pg_write_server_files`). Migrating also refuses an application login named after one of the server's roles or with PostgreSQL's reserved `pg_` prefix, and, when the server logs in with a password, one whose `VALID UNTIL` has passed. How the maintenance pool logs in, including its grant of `CONNECT`, is left to item 11. Recorded in PRD 1.6 §5.4 and §10.8. | 5, 6, 9, 11 |
| N17 | PRD §10.3 serves `/metrics` "unauthenticated on the admin port" in self-hosted mode and "protected" in hosted mode, without saying where the admin port is or what protects it. NFR-SEC-09 sends HSTS "in hosted mode", and FR-ADMIN-04 AC3 does not say how an instance whose startup migration failed refuses traffic. | Decided: the admin port is a second listener, `STORYCREEP_ADMIN_ADDR` (`127.0.0.1:9090` by default, or `off`), that serves only `/metrics`. Protected means a bearer token, `STORYCREEP_METRICS_TOKEN`, and setting the token protects `/metrics` in self-hosted mode too. Without a token, hosted mode serves no metrics and opens no admin port, which FR-ADMIN-05 AC3 allows ("disabled or protected"). HSTS is sent whenever `STORYCREEP_PUBLIC_URL` is https, which hosted mode requires, as the `Secure` cookie attribute follows the URL (NFR-SEC-04). An instance whose startup migration fails closes its listeners and exits with status 1; until its migrations are applied, `/readyz` and the API answer 503. No PRD change. | 6, 9, 115 |
| N18 | FR-ADMIN-01 AC4 allows no build toolchain in the image, and FR-ADMIN-07 asks for a health check of the server, which an image without a shell or an HTTP client cannot run. FR-ADMIN-07 AC1 allows no configuration beyond a database password, but the server needs two database logins (N16) and a public URL. AC2 publishes images before any release exists. | Decided: the image is the binary on distroless `static`, running as the non-root user 65532, and the binary gains a `healthcheck` subcommand, which requests `/readyz`, because FR-ADMIN-06's list of subcommands is a minimum. The compose file's one required setting is `POSTGRES_PASSWORD`, which both logins share through `PGPASSWORD`. It publishes the server on `127.0.0.1:7777`, with `http://localhost:7777` as the public URL, for a reverse proxy on the same host that terminates TLS (§10.8). Until the first release, every CI run on the main branch, after a push and weekly, publishes `ghcr.io/cybercute-studio/storycreep` from its commit, tagged `sha-<commit>`, and `main` unless `main` has a newer commit already, and the compose file runs `main`; item 119 adds release tags and points the compose file at the release. The compose file mounts the asset volume at `/var/lib/storycreep/assets`, which item 29's local driver uses in the image. No PRD change. | 9, 29, 119 |
| N19 | FR-I18N-02 resolves a locale from "user preference → `Accept-Language` → instance default → English" without saying where the instance default is set, and FR-I18N-01 localises "API error messages intended for display" without saying which parts of the error envelope those are. Nor does it say whether the server and the web app format ICU messages alike. | Decided: the instance default is the configuration key `STORYCREEP_DEFAULT_LOCALE` (`en` by default), which accepts only a locale that the server has a catalog for. The envelope's `message` is localised, and the response's `Content-Language` names its language; `details`, including the validation messages in `details.errors`, is for programs and stays in English. The server formats a subset of ICU MessageFormat (no dates or times yet), held to the web app's i18next-icu by shared vectors in `/schema/i18n`, and the web app formats dates and relative times with `Intl`. No PRD change. | 10, 18, 54, 120 |
| N20 | N16 left to item 11 how the maintenance pool logs in. PostgreSQL does not pass `BYPASSRLS` on to the members of a role, and the application login must not be a member of `storycreep_maintenance` (§5.4), so the pool needs a login of its own that acts as the role with `SET ROLE`. | Decided: a third database URL, `STORYCREEP_DATABASE_MAINTENANCE_URL`, optional until the first maintenance job needs it, which is the first of items 84, 110, 111, 112, and 114 to land, and which then makes the server require it. When it is set, migrating provisions its login as N16 provisions the application's: it creates or updates it with the password it logs in with, sent as a SCRAM verifier, grants it `storycreep_maintenance` and `CONNECT` on the database, and refuses a login named after the server's roles or with the `pg_` prefix, one that is the application login or the admin login, and one with more privileges than the role gives it: a superuser, a role with `BYPASSRLS` itself, `CREATEROLE`, or `REPLICATION`, an owner, or a member of `storycreep_app`, `storycreep_definer`, a role that gives access to the database server's files and programs, or `pg_write_all_data`. Both logins are refused as members of `pg_write_all_data`, which may change the rows of every table past the migrations' grants, on which the application's lack of `DELETE` on `user` and `workspace`, and of `UPDATE` and `DELETE` on `audit_entry`, rests. It then connects as the login and fails unless it can act as the role and reaches a migrated database. Every transaction of the pool runs `SET LOCAL ROLE storycreep_maintenance`. Because the maintenance logins of all Storycreep databases on a server are members of `storycreep_maintenance`, migrating revokes `CONNECT` from all of the server's roles, not only `storycreep_app`, and a server that does not migrate refuses a database that any of them can connect to. The compose files set the URL to the login `storycreep_maintainer`, which shares `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` through `PGPASSWORD` (N18). Recorded in PRD 1.7 §5.4 and §10.8. | 11, 84, 110, 111, 112, 114 |
| N21 | FR-TEN-07 lists events that belong to no workspace, such as a login, a session revocation, and an account's deletion, and §5.4 asks each maintenance run, some of which span workspaces, for an audit entry, but FR-TEN-01 AC1 made `audit_entry.workspace_id` non-null under row-level security, so none of them could be recorded. §9.6 asks for a keyed hash of a failed login's unknown address without saying where the key comes from, and FR-ADMIN-07 AC1 allows no configuration beyond a database password. | Decided: `audit_entry.workspace_id` is null for an event that belongs to no workspace. A policy of its own lets the application add such an entry only from a transaction without a workspace, and no policy lets it read one yet; item 87 adds what the personal-data export needs, and the maintenance jobs read and delete them past row-level security. The hash is HMAC-SHA256 of the address in lower case, as `citext` compares it, with a random 32-byte key that the migration generates once for the database and keeps in a new cross-workspace table, `instance_key`, which the application may read but not change. Nothing needs configuring, and a backup and its restore keep the key with the hashes; a copy of the whole database holds both, which the PRD records. Recorded in PRD 1.8 FR-TEN-01, FR-TEN-07, §5.3, §5.4, and §9.6. | 12, 14, 87, 111, 112 |
| N22 | NFR-SEC-06 and FR-AUTH-05 limit requests per IP address, and FR-AUTH-02 lists each session's IP address, but the documented deployments put a reverse proxy in front of the server: the compose file publishes on `127.0.0.1:7777` for a proxy on the same host (N18), and hosted mode runs behind a load balancer (§10.8). Every request's connection then comes from the proxy, so all clients would share one address's limits, and one client could use up the limit of signing in for everyone. | Decided: a configuration key, `STORYCREEP_TRUSTED_PROXIES`, lists the addresses and CIDR prefixes that proxies connect from, none by default. A request whose connection comes from one of them is taken to come from the rightmost address in its `X-Forwarded-For` header that is not a trusted proxy, and any other request from its connection's address. That address is what the per-IP limits count, an IPv6 address by its /64, and what sessions and the audit log record. `Forwarded` (RFC 7239) is not read, because the proxies in common use set `X-Forwarded-For`. The compose file passes the key through, and the deploy README recommends Docker's default range of network addresses for a proxy that reaches the published port or the compose network. No PRD change. | 14, 15 |
| N23 | FR-AUTH-05 asks for exponential backoff and a temporary account lock after 10 consecutive failed sign-ins without saying how long a lock lasts or what backs off, and NFR-SEC-06 names the buckets of the rate limits without their limits. | Decided: each sign-in with an email address counts as a failure until it succeeds, which forgets the failures (FR-AUTH-05 AC3). The 10th in a row locks the address for a minute, and each failure after a lock ends locks it for twice as long as the lock before, up to 15 minutes; failures are forgotten 24 hours after the last one. The lock belongs to the address, folded to lower case as `citext` compares it, whether or not an account has it, so that a lock does not tell whether one does (FR-AUTH-01). A sign-in with a locked address is refused before its password is checked, with 429 `rate_limited` and `details.reason` `locked`, and is audited as a failed login with that reason. The limits are constants in the code. A minute: auth 20 per IP address, 10 per user, and 10 per email address that a registration names (signing in is limited by the lock of its address instead); reads 1,200 per IP address and 600 per user; writes 600 and 300; WebSocket connects 60 and 30. An hour: exports 60 and 30, and invitation emails 10 per mailbox, whose addresses that differ only in a +tag, in how their domain is written, or, at Gmail, in dots, count as one, from all workspaces together, which the email job counts (item 19, N27). A bucket counts fixed windows from the Unix epoch, in PostgreSQL with the database's clock, and holds its keys as HMAC-SHA256 with a key that migrating generates in `instance_key` (N21). Item 15 attaches the auth bucket to signing in, registering, and changing a password; items 18 and 26 attach it to password reset and token creation, item 26 adds the token key, and items 27 and 52 attach the other buckets. No PRD change, but for the bucket of invitation emails, which PRD 1.12 adds to NFR-SEC-06 (N27). | 15, 18, 19, 26, 27, 52 |
| N24 | PRD §5.5 resolves an instance admin to `owner` of every workspace and project when "mode = self-hosted and instance_admin_access enabled", but nothing defined that switch, and no code outside the configuration may read the mode (FR-ADMIN-01 AC3). FR-TEN-01 gives every user a personal workspace on first login without saying whether an account that already belongs to a workspace by then, as an invited one does, gets one too. Item 16's "retention placeholders" overlap item 112, which makes retention configurable. | Decided: the switch is the capability `InstanceAdminAccess`, from a new key, `STORYCREEP_INSTANCE_ADMIN_ACCESS`, `off` by default and an error in hosted mode, so that an operator opts in. Every account gets a personal workspace once, at its first sign-in, whatever it belongs to by then, named after its display name. Item 16 reserves a `retention` object in a workspace's stored settings and shows it nowhere; item 112 adds the retention settings to the API together with the jobs that apply them, so that the API never states a retention that nothing applies. Recorded in PRD 1.9 §5.5 and §10.3. | 16, 17, 112, 114 |
| N25 | FR-ADMIN-02 has first-run setup choose the signup policy, but §10.3 resolved `Signup` once at startup from configuration, so a choice made on a running instance had nowhere to go. Setup's optional SMTP and storage steps would configure settings that nothing reads before items 18 and 29. Until setup completes, whoever first reaches a new instance could complete it and make themselves its administrator. And every account gets a personal workspace at its first sign-in (N24), so the setup owner would get one besides the workspace that setup creates. | Decided: setup stores the signup policy that it chooses, `closed` by default, in a new cross-workspace table, `instance`, whose one row also records when setup completed, and `STORYCREEP_SIGNUP` overrides it for as long as it is set: without the key, the self-hosted `Signup` capability is `setup`, and registering reads the stored policy. Completing setup through `POST /api/v1/setup` needs a setup code, 80 bits of a key that migrating generates in `instance_key`, which the server logs while setup is pending. Unattended setup, from the `STORYCREEP_SETUP_` keys, needs none, and runs before the server is ready. The setup owner is an instance admin, and the setup workspace is the owner's personal workspace. A database that has accounts when it is upgraded counts as set up. The SMTP step lands in item 18 and the storage step in item 29, with the settings that they configure. Recorded in PRD 1.10 FR-ADMIN-02, FR-AUTH-01, §5.3, and §10.3. | 17, 18, 29 |
| N26 | FR-AUTH-03 surfaces links as text to instance admins without saying how they read them, and FR-ADMIN-02's SMTP step had no settings to record, while hosted mode must always send email (`EmailRequired`). A link whose secret is stored so that an admin can read it later could be used by whoever reads the database, and a reset request that sends its email itself would take longer for an address of an account (FR-AUTH-03 AC3). | Decided: the SMTP server that setup chooses is stored in the `instance` row, its password as it is, because the mailer must send it; `STORYCREEP_SMTP_URL` and `STORYCREEP_SMTP_FROM` override it while they are set, hosted mode requires them, and unattended setup records the server of `STORYCREEP_SETUP_SMTP_URL` and `STORYCREEP_SETUP_SMTP_FROM`. A link's token is stored as the SHA-256 hash of its secret, in a new cross-workspace table, `email_token`, and an emailed link's secret is made by the job that sends it, so that neither the request nor the job's arguments hold it, and the link lasts its lifetime from then, as its email says, while the job sends nothing for a request older than that lifetime. A reset request, and a registration on an instance that sends email, adds a token and a job whether or not the address has an account, and the job deletes a token without one. The job emails an account one link of a kind in 10 minutes at most: it sends nothing for a link of an account that was emailed another of that kind in the last 10 minutes which still works, as `email_token.sent_at` tells, and it locks the account first, so that jobs of one account's links that run at once see each other's emails. The request still adds its token and its job and answers 202, so that it does the same work whatever the address (FR-AUTH-03 AC3), and the earlier email's link keeps working, while a reset request every few seconds sends six emails an hour at most rather than the 600 that the address's rate limit lets through. An email that no attempt could deliver, because the SMTP server refuses its recipient's mailbox for good (a reply whose enhanced status code is 5.1.1 to 5.1.4, 5.1.6, 5.1.10, or 5.2.1), or the recipient's local part, or a domain without an ASCII form, needs SMTPUTF8, which the server lacks, cancels its job rather than retry it for a day (`jobs.Cancel`), and a cancelled job is in the dead-letter state, as a discarded one is, for 7 days (FR-JOBS-01 AC3); a full mailbox, a refusal of a relay or of the sender that a server gives in its reply to RCPT, as Postfix does by default, and a refusal without an enhanced status code, which cannot tell a mailbox from a relay or a sender, as Exim's `550 relay not permitted`, are retried, because the mailbox may empty and the operator can mend the settings. Without SMTP, a request for an emailed link answers 403 `email_not_configured` for every address, and an instance admin takes a new link with `POST /api/v1/admin/password-reset-links` or `/api/v1/admin/verification-links`, which answer 403 `email_configured` on an instance that sends email; `storycreep reset-password` prints one either way, in hosted mode too, where it is the only copyable link. `GET /api/v1/instance` is the banner flag. A workspace needs a verified address exactly when `EmailRequired` is on, and the verification gives the personal workspace that the first sign-in could not. A reset revokes every session of the account. Links open `/app/reset-password` and `/app/verify-email` with the token in the URL's fragment, which a browser does not send to the server. Recorded in PRD 1.11 FR-AUTH-03, §5.3, and §9.6. | 18, 54, 114 |
| N27 | FR-TEN-02 left open whether the `closed` signup policy lets an invitation create an account, how someone without an account accepts one, what resending does, what an emailed invitation says, and who sees the member list. Item 20 alone required `owner` for inviting an owner and removing one, while item 19 adds both operations. | Decided: under `closed`, an invitation creates no account, so only an account that exists already accepts one; under `invite` and `open`, `POST /api/v1/invites/register` registers an account with the link and accepts the invitation in the transaction that adds it, answering 202 alike whether or not the address has an account (FR-AUTH-01 AC1), which then stays as it was, with the invitation open. A signed-in user accepts with `POST /api/v1/invites/accept`, which answers the account that accepted an invitation with its membership again, so a client registers, signs in, and accepts either way. An invitation to an address is emailed where the instance sends email, its secret made by the job that sends it, as N26's links are, and its 14 days start then; otherwise its link goes as text to the admin who makes or resends it, and a link invitation's always does. The email names the instance and the role, never the workspace or the inviting user, whose names their users chose, so that no account can put text of its own in email that the instance sends to any address; `POST /api/v1/invites/lookup` shows the workspace to the link's holder. Every member with a role in the workspace, which a guest's is not (§5.5), lists the members and their roles; only owners and admins see their email addresses and the invitations. An address has one open invitation of a workspace, one neither accepted nor revoked, expired or not (409 `invite_exists`). Resending gives a new link and 14 days more, and the old link stops working at once. A workspace emails an address one invitation in 10 minutes at most, as N26 emails an account one link of a kind: the job of an invitation made or resent sooner after the workspace last emailed the address is due once those 10 minutes have passed, and sends the latest link, so that resending again and again, or revoking and inviting again, cannot flood an inbox at the 10 a minute that the `auth` bucket lets an account make. Nor can an account that owns many workspaces, or invites many addresses of one mailbox: the instance emails a mailbox 10 invitations at most in each clock hour (UTC), from all workspaces together, counted in a new bucket of the rate limits, `invite_email`, keyed by the mailbox alone (N23): the address without a +tag, with its domain in the ASCII form that the instance sends to, without the dot that a full stop at its end leaves, so that the spellings of a domain, as in full-width letters or full stops, which the instance delivers to one domain, are one, and, at Gmail, whose gmail.com and googlemail.com are one, without the dots that Gmail ignores, so that jo+1@example.com, jo+2@example.com, and so on, which most mail services deliver to one inbox, share one limit. The limit is above the 6 that one workspace can send an address in an hour. The email's job counts it once the job knows that it has an email to send, unless an earlier attempt of the job gave the invitation its link, which only an attempt that counted the email does, so that the retries of an email that an SMTP server that is down refused, or that the server's stop cut off, do not use up the mailbox's hour, while an attempt that failed before its count leaves the email to be counted by the next; a retry in a later hour than its count sends the email past that hour's 10, as once an SMTP server that was down for hours takes email again. The job of an email over the limit sends nothing and ends, cancelled with the limit as its error, so that the job's row and metrics tell the operator why: the invitation stays without a link, as one still to be emailed, and its admins send it anew later, which the maintainer chose over putting the email off in a queue that the invitation would hold a turn in. Refusing the request instead would tell one workspace that others invited the address. A revoked invitation keeps its row, so that its link answers 410. Removing a member revokes the workspace's open invitations that the member made, those whose link the member was last given as text, by making or resending them, which `invite.link_holder` records, and those to the member's address, recording each revocation with the removal's actor, so that no link that the member holds, as an admin holds a link invitation's, lets them back in. A demotion that takes away the right to manage members does the same from item 20, which adds role changes; until then, only the operator's `grant-role` demotes. Item 19 answers 403 `owner_required` to an admin who invites an owner, resends such an invitation, or removes an owner, and item 20 adds role changes; item 19 tests FR-TEN-02 AC2 over removal, and item 20 over demotion. Making and resending invitations, and reading, accepting, and registering with a link, share the `auth` bucket (N23). Recorded in PRD 1.12 FR-AUTH-01, FR-AUTH-03, FR-TEN-02, NFR-SEC-06, §5.3, and §5.6. | 19, 20, 54, 55 |
| N28 | FR-TEN-02 and N27 said what removing a member revokes, and that a demotion that takes away the right to manage members does the same, but not what a role change does to the invitations that the member made or holds the link of whose role the member may still give, nor to those as `owner` of an owner made `admin`, who could still let in an owner with them. | Decided: a role change, by `PATCH /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/members/{membership_id}` or the operator's `grant-role`, revokes the open invitations, expired or not, that the member made or was last given the link of as text whose role the new role may not give: every one, for a role below `admin`, and those as `owner`, for `admin`, recording each with the change's actor, or none for the operator's, and the membership. It revokes them before it changes the membership, as a removal does before it deletes one, so that an acceptance by the member at that moment is refused at once rather than deadlock with it. A change to the role that the membership has already changes and records nothing, and an admin is refused any change of an owner's membership with `owner_required`, as making a member owner is. The route answers with the member, with their email address. The service-layer authorisation package is `internal/authz`, which holds §5.6 for the operations that exist, with the two rows that manage members as two operations, and from which every service checks a role; listing the members gets a capability of its own. The viewer test of FR-TEN-03 AC3 reads its reads as those that §5.6 lets a viewer make: a viewer's list of the invitations, which §5.6 shows only to owners and admins (N27), is answered with 403 `insufficient_role`, as every change is. Recorded in PRD 1.13 FR-TEN-02 and §5.3. | 20 |
| N29 | FR-ENT-01 gives self-hosted mode "optional instance-level caps" without saying how they are set, and asks for one plan file that the server, the pricing page, and the tests read without saying where it lives or how it is written. FR-ENT-03 asks for an upgrade link without naming its page. Item 21 lands before usage metering (item 23), so nothing reads a workspace's usage yet. | Decided: the plan file is `server/internal/entitlements/plans.json`, which the server embeds, because a Go module embeds only the files under its own directory, and the image is built from `server/` alone; the pricing page (FR-PUB-07) reads it from there. It is JSON: the limits, in order, `members` marked `billable` and `snapshot_retention` marked `inert_until` FR-SNAP-06; the features; and each plan's quota of every limit, a whole number or `"unlimited"`, and whether it has every feature. The server refuses a file whose limits that are not inert, or whose features, are not the ones that its code names, or that lacks the `unlimited` or the `free` plan. While `EnforceEntitlements` is on, a workspace has the plan that its `plan_key` names, `free` for a new one, and otherwise every workspace has `unlimited`. The caps come from a new configuration key, `STORYCREEP_WORKSPACE_LIMITS`, such as `members=20, projects=50`: a whole number of at least 1 for each limit that it names, which caps every workspace, whatever its plan allows. Hosted mode refuses the key, because there each workspace's plan sets its limits. `Check` reads usage in the transaction of the change, through a meter that item 23 provides. `409 limit_exceeded` carries `details.key`, `details.usage`, and `details.limit`, and `details.upgrade_url`, the public site's `/pricing`, only where the `Billing` and `PublicSite` capabilities are both on, the plan rather than a cap sets the limit, and another plan allows more. No PRD change. | 21, 23 |
| N30 | FR-API-03 and §10.4 left open where a command carries `expected_rev`, what a batch's revisions are checked against, and what a stale revision of an entity that no longer exists answers, and §5.3 made `applied_command.command_id` a primary key of its own, which a command ID that a client reused in another project, or that a client of another workspace sent, would collide with, revealing that the ID exists. Nor did they say how a project's `seq` stays in order, what an `operation` row of several commands holds, or how the plan's limits are checked for a batch. | Decided: a command carries `expected_rev` beside its payload, as a map from the ID of each entity that it changes or refers to, of a kind that its registration touches, to the revision that the client authored it against, so that the pipeline checks every type of command alike, and the review queue's keep-mine (FR-OFF-04) sets the current revision without knowing the payload. Every `expected_rev` of a batch is checked against the entities as they are before the batch, before any of its commands applies, so an entity that the batch creates has none to expect. A stale one refuses the batch with 409 `revision_conflict`, whose `details.conflicts` lists every stale entity of every command, with the command's index, ID, and type, the expected revision, and the entity's current revision and state, or `exists` false and neither for an entity that no longer exists, as one purged from the trash or never created (FR-OFF-04 AC5). A command ID is unique within its project: `applied_command`'s primary key is its workspace, its project, and the command ID. The pipeline is `internal/commands`. It validates each payload against its type's schema with the validator of the API's request bodies, so that a payload that it accepts is one that item 28's OpenAPI document describes, and a payload's type has no field with a default, nor a type that resolves itself, which the API would give a request's body but a payload never gets. It resolves the actor's effective role on the project and their role in its workspace, answers 404 when the project does not exist, when its workspace's deletion is pending (FR-TEN-05 AC1), and when the effective role is none, and authorises each command by the role that its operation's scope names (§5.5). It then locks the project's row, so that a project's batches are applied one at a time and their seqs commit in order without a gap, which the catch-up of items 52 and 92 relies on, checks each limit once for what all of the batch's commands add to it, reads the revisions of each kind's entities that the batch expects in one query, and applies the commands in order, each one's inverse computed in the batch's transaction right before it applies, unless a command of the batch cannot be undone, which leaves the batch without one. `project.seq` is the seq of the project's latest operation, 0 before its first. An `operation` row's `type` is its command's type, or `batch` for several commands, its `payload` the commands with their IDs, types, and payloads, its `inverse` their inverses from the last command to the first, or null if one of them cannot be undone, and its `entity_refs` the entities that the batch changed, with their revisions after it, which the batch's result returns too. The post-commit `NOTIFY` hook is a `Notifier`, which nothing implements until item 52. The metrics are `storycreep_command_batches_total` and `storycreep_command_batch_duration_seconds` by result, and `storycreep_commands_applied_total` and `storycreep_command_revision_conflicts_total` by the command's type. No operation of §5.6 is about a project's content yet, so item 22 tests the pipeline with commands of its tests' own on a table of their own, and item 24 registers the first real ones, with the project's `rev`. A change of a project's content outside a batch, such as the purge of the trash (item 31), takes the project's lock as a batch does. A batch reads the actor's membership and grant without locking them, so that a change of either that commits once the batch has read them, as while it waits for the project's lock, is ordered after the batch, and a removal or a role change, which locks the membership FOR UPDATE while it holds the workspace's row, never waits for the member's batches: PostgreSQL grants a lock FOR SHARE at once even while such a lock waits, so batches that locked the membership so, one overlapping the next, would put the change off for as long as the member kept sending them, and every change of the workspace's members and settings with it. Recorded in PRD 1.14 FR-API-03, §5.3, and §5.7. | 22, 24, 28, 31, 37, 52, 92 |
| N31 | FR-ENT-02 counts API requests per workspace, which item 21 left to item 23, but item 23 said nothing of how, and a workspace's API traffic means something only once API tokens exist (item 26). Nor did FR-ENT-02 say how the exact mode serializes changes, how reconciliation corrects drift while only the rollup job writes the counters, whether a guest counts as a member, who reads the usage, how often the jobs run, or what registering with the link of a workspace at its members limit answers. | Decided: item 27 meters API requests, with the requests of API tokens, as an `api_requests` key. Package `internal/usage` meters `projects`, `boards`, `nodes`, `members`, `storage_bytes`, and `snapshots`, and a workspace's usage of a key is its counter's value plus the sum of its deltas still present, read in one statement. The entitlement layer's meter takes a transaction-scoped advisory lock of the workspace's key before it reads a key that is counted exactly (`projects`, `members`, `snapshots`), so that the changes that check the key's limit take turns, and no lock for the others. The rollup job runs every 5 minutes and the reconciliation job every hour, each over the workspaces of the `workspace` table, in a transaction of each workspace (§5.4), not on the maintenance path. A run starts at a workspace picked at random, goes on past the last to the first, and starts no workspace once it has run for 40 seconds, well inside the minute that a job has, so that on an instance with more workspaces than that, a run still ends in time, and the workspaces that one run does not reach, another does. Reconciliation recounts each key that a recount is registered for, under the key's lock, and records the drift as a delta, so that the rollup job stays the only writer of the counters; each item that records a key's deltas registers its recount (`members` in item 23). Every membership counts towards `members`, guests' included, the migration records the memberships that exist then as a delta, and every plan allows at least one member, the owner. A command reports what it adds with `Tx.AddUsage`, which the pipeline records as one delta of each key with the batch, and which refuses to add to a limit that the command does not declare, and the pipeline refuses a batch that adds more to a limit than its commands declared, which is all that it checked the limit for. The usage endpoint is `GET /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/usage`, whose body has a property of each key, so that a new key is a property that clients ignore, and which the "Workspace settings" row of §5.6 authorises. Registering with the link of an invitation to an address of a workspace at its members limit adds the account but not the membership, and answers 202, as for an address that has an account (FR-AUTH-01 AC1), and the account's acceptance once it signs in answers 409 `limit_exceeded`. A link invitation registers any address, so one left open so would register account after account under the `invite` policy: registering with its link answers 409 `limit_exceeded` while the workspace is full, whatever the address, from a check of the members limit (`workspace.Service.CheckRoom`, which `addMembership` makes too) made before the address is looked at, and made again in the transaction that would add the account, under the lock of the workspace's members, for an address that has an account as for a new one, so that registrations that the link lets through while the workspace fills up add no account either, and the answer still does not tell whether the address has one. That transaction checks the invitation again for an address that has an account too, so that one accepted or revoked meanwhile is answered alike for both. Recorded in PRD 1.15 FR-TEN-02 and FR-AUTH-01. | 23, 24, 27, 29, 30, 35, 78 |
| N32 | FR-PROJ-01 and §5.6 left open how a project is created, when a batch of commands applies to a project that exists, which row of §5.6 covers renaming, describing, archiving, and unarchiving a project, restoring it from the trash, and listing a workspace's projects and its trash, what counts towards `projects`, and what a project in the trash answers until it is purged. Nor did §5.3 say what a project's default locale may be, or what its entry may name before boards and nodes exist. | Decided: creating a project is `project.Service.Create`, not a command, and the one site that creates a project, which checks the `projects` limit, and which importing a project goes through too (item 84). Every later change of a project is a command of the project as an entity, whose `rev` an `expected_rev` names (N30): `project.rename`, `project.describe`, `project.update_settings` (the default locale, `publish_min_role`, and the entry), `project.archive`, and `project.unarchive`, which "Change project settings" authorises (P), and `project.delete` and `project.restore`, which "Create/delete projects" authorises (W), as it does listing the trash; each of a pair undoes the other. The routes are `GET` and `POST /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/projects`, `GET /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/trash/projects`, `GET`, `PATCH`, and `DELETE /api/v1/projects/{project_id}`, and `POST /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/archive`, `…/unarchive`, and `…/restore`. The list shows a member the projects on which their effective role is not `none`, so a guest sees those granted to them. A type of command declares whether it applies to an archived project (`Spec.Archived`: unarchiving, deleting, restoring) and to one in the trash (`Spec.Trashed`: restoring), and the pipeline refuses any other batch of an archived project with 409 `project_archived`, and of one in the trash with 404, which it reads before the actor's role, so that a refusal of the role does not tell that the project exists, and again once it holds the project's lock. A project counts towards `projects` while it is neither archived nor in the trash, so unarchiving and restoring, which make it count again, check the limit too, as the pipeline checks the limits that a command declares, for which `Limit.Adds` reads the project in the batch's transaction. FR-ENT-01 AC1's one enforcement site of a limit is read, for `projects`, as one for each change that makes a project count: `Create` for a new project, and the pipeline's check of the limit that `project.unarchive` and `project.restore` declare, each with a test that reaches the limit, which item 117's entitlement check counts as such. A deleted project stays in the trash for 30 days, during which every route but its restoration answers it with 404, and then every route does; an hourly purge job deletes it, one project in each transaction of its workspace, skipping a project whose row a batch holds, and records `project.purge` without an actor. Every table with a `project_id` has a foreign key, with its `workspace_id`, to the project, `ON DELETE CASCADE`, which a schema test checks, so that the purge deletes every row of a project (standing checklist). The jobs that work on each workspace share package `sweep`. The default locale is a BCP 47 tag of a language, kept in its canonical form, of at most 64 characters as sent and in that form, and the workspace's unless the project names one; the entry's board and node are IDs of their kinds, which items 30 and 35 check exist, and the validation reports one that no longer does (FR-DEP-04). The audit log records `project.create`, `project.delete`, `project.restore`, `project.purge`, and `project.publish_policy_change`. The project's slug of §5.3, which only the published format reads, lands with the boards' slugs in item 30. Recorded in PRD 1.16 §5.6. | 24, 30, 35, 84, 117 |
| N33 | FR-TEN-04 and §5.6 left open how grants are addressed and changed, who may change an owner's grant, which replaces the owner's role on the project as a demotion would, what a grant of `owner` answers beside the 400 of AC5, what happens to the grants of an archived project and of one in the trash, and what the audit log records. AC4 said that a `guest` with an `admin` grant receives 403 on every workspace-scoped operation, while §5.5 answers a resolved role of `none`, which a guest's workspace role is, with 404. | Decided: a grant has no public ID, because a membership has one on a project at most, so its routes name the project and the membership: `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/grants` lists a project's grants, `PUT /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/grants/{membership_id}` adds a grant or changes its role, and one with the role already stays as it is, `DELETE` on the same path removes it, and `GET /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/members/{membership_id}/grants` lists a member's grants on the workspace's projects, all of them under "Manage project grants" (W), which owners and admins of the workspace hold, never a grant. The routes of a project answer 404 when the project does not exist, is in the trash, or the user's effective role on it is `none`, as the project's other routes do, and 403 when their workspace role does not allow it. Only an `owner` sets or removes the grant of an owner's membership, and an admin receives 403 `owner_required`, as for a demotion (FR-TEN-03 AC4), which the maintainer chose. A grant of `owner` answers 400 `owner_not_grantable`, a new error code, so `owner` stays in the request's enum, where the schema would answer it with 422; `guest` and anything else answer 422. The grants of an archived project change, because they are who may act on it, not its content, and those of a project in the trash are kept, and apply again once it is restored, but nothing lists or changes them meanwhile. Removing a membership, and purging a project, delete their grants by the cascade of the foreign keys. A change of a grant holds the workspace's lock, which every change of a membership's role and every removal holds too, so that the member keeps the role that the change was checked against. The audit log records `project_grant.create`, `project_grant.change`, and `project_grant.remove`, about the membership, with the project in `project_id`. AC4 is read as §5.5 has it, which the maintainer chose over answering guests 403 on every route of the workspace: the guest is refused every workspace-scoped operation, with 404 on the workspace's routes and 403 on the routes of the granted project itself, which the grant shows to them, and the viewer with 403. | N34 | FR-AUTH-04 and NFR-SEC-05 left open where tokens are managed, how a route declares that it takes a token, and what a token gets on a route that takes none before item 27's gate; what removing the owner's membership does to the owner's tokens; how a request presents a token beside a session; and how the limits of the token key relate to the owner's, which N23 left to item 26. The session-only endpoints named token creation and revocation, but not the listing of tokens. | Decided: a token of the whole workspace is made, listed, and revoked with `POST` and `GET /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/tokens` and `DELETE /api/v1/workspaces/{workspace_id}/tokens/{token_id}`, under "Manage own workspace-wide API tokens" (W), and a token narrowed to a project with the same routes under `/api/v1/projects/{project_id}/tokens`, under "Manage own API tokens narrowed to this project" (P), which a guest's `editor` grant gives; other members' tokens on either under "Manage API tokens (others')" (W), which no grant gives. A list shows its tokens that are not revoked, every member's to owners and admins, and one's own to the rest. All six routes are session-only, and making a token counts in the auth bucket (N23). The secret is `scp_` and 32 random bytes in base64url, of which the database holds the SHA-256 hash, and only the answer that makes it holds it. A route declares the scope that it takes a token with when it is registered; in item 26, reading a project takes `project:read`, and changing its name, description, and settings, archiving it, and unarchiving it take `project:write`, and every other route that needs a signed-in user answers a valid token 403 `session_required` already, which item 27 then makes every route declare. A request presents a token as `Authorization: Bearer scp_…`, which needs no CSRF token and takes precedence over a cookie; a credential of another scheme, or a bearer credential without the prefix, is ignored, so that a proxy's own authentication keeps working. A token that is unknown, revoked, or expired, of a workspace whose deletion is pending, or whose owner is not active or is no longer a member of its workspace, answers 401 `unauthenticated` with RFC 6750's `WWW-Authenticate`, and one without the route's scope 403 `insufficient_scope`, which names it, in its details and in that header; a route that takes a token sends the scheme's challenge with every 401, to a request without credentials too. A refusal of a token that exists is audited as `token.use_denied`, with its reason, at most once a minute for each token, as its `last_used_at` is written, so that a client that repeats a refused request, as a CI job left with a revoked token, adds an entry a minute to the audit log rather than one a request. The services and the pipeline answer a project that a token does not cover with 404, and the pipeline refuses a batch of a token without the scope that each of its commands declares when it is registered, `project:write` for every command of a project so far, and names that scope. Removing a member revokes their tokens of the workspace in the removal's transaction, audited as `token.revoke` with the membership, so that a member who rejoins does not get them back, which the maintainer chose over refusing them only while their owner is not a member. A token's requests count against the token's key, which each bucket allows as many requests as a user, and against its owner's, so that many tokens make no more requests than one user may, which the maintainer chose. `storycreep create-token` makes a token for a member of a workspace, as the operator, without an actor in the audit log, and prints its secret alone on standard output. Changing a project's settings, archiving, and unarchiving keep `project:write`. A route declares `api.TokenScope` or `api.SessionOnly`, and `api.Register` refuses one that declares neither or both; `TestRouteClassification` lists the routes that take a token. Each route counts in one bucket, which its declaration names: `auth` where items 15, 18, 19, and 26 put it, and for listing and revoking API tokens, in which `api.Register` refuses a route that takes a token, so that a leaked token's requests, which count against its owner too, cannot use up the owner's limit of the requests that find and revoke it, after which the authenticator refuses its requests before any limit counts them; and otherwise `read` for a GET and `write` for the rest, signing out and listing and revoking sessions among them, so that the sign-ins of an IP address's other users and the account's other requests about credentials, which share the auth bucket's small limits, do not refuse them; `export` is attached by the export routes (items 79, 82, 83, 86). `api.Register` attaches the limit after the route's own guards, appends the limit to the route's description, and documents the RateLimit headers on its responses, and it refuses a route of the API, under `/api/`, that no bucket counts: only `/healthz`, `/readyz`, `/version`, the pages, and `/metrics`, which answer without the database, in which the limits count, count in none. It has no recount, because nothing but its deltas records the requests, so reconciliation leaves it alone. The one large read so far is the OpenAPI document, which has its `ETag` since item 7; the routes of board content, snapshots, and exports add theirs with `api.ETag`. | N36 | FR-API-03 said how a batch's commands are idempotent, but not what a request of the command endpoint and its answer look like, how the multi-batch form treats a batch that is not valid, how many batches and commands a request may hold, which rejections an idempotency record remembers, what a batch whose commands were all applied but grouped otherwise answers, where the check runs relative to the project's lock and its trashed and archived states, or how the records are kept and pruned. Nor did item 28 say how the OpenAPI document describes the commands, which N30 left to it. | Decided: a command is `{"command_id", "type", "payload", "expected_rev"}`, `command_id` a UUID and `expected_rev` keyed by the entities' public IDs, which package commands reads (`Pipeline.Parse`), so that every problem of a batch, of its JSON and of its payloads alike, is one `validation_failed` located in the body, as `body.batches[1].commands[0].payload.name`. The document describes a command as one of a schema for each registered type, named after it, as `ProjectRenameCommand`, with its payload's schema, `ProjectRenamePayload`, built from the payload's type as the pipeline's is (`Registry.Document`), so that registering a type changes the document, and the TypeScript types are a union of the commands that their `type` tells apart. The endpoint takes `{"commands": [...]}`, answered with `{status, seq, revisions}` or the batch's error, or `{"batches": [{"commands": [...]}, ...]}`, answered with 200 and `{results}`, each `applied`, `already_applied`, or `rejected` with the envelope's object of its error and `Content-Language`; huma validates only the body's form, with a schema that takes any batch, and refuses a body that is not JSON with 400 `invalid_request`, or one of another type with 415 `unsupported_media_type`, as on every route, while the document describes the body's full schema; the handler and the pipeline validate each batch and its commands, so that a batch that is not valid, or cannot be read, is rejected alone, and its keys and its commands' are matched exactly (`commands.DecodeObject`), as huma's validation matches the other bodies' (FR-API-01 AC2). A command may expect a revision of the batch's own project only, and one of another project makes the batch invalid, where the check of the revisions would take it for a project that no longer exists. A `revision_conflict`'s details hold the state of each stale entity that still exists once, in `details.states`, by its ID, rather than with each command's conflict in `details.conflicts`, as N30 had it, so that a batch of many commands that expect one entity's revision is neither answered nor remembered with the entity's state once for each. A batch that an API token sends with `project.delete` or `project.restore`, which FR-AUTH-04 makes session-only, is rejected with 403 `session_required`, whatever its scopes (`Spec.SessionOnly`), so that a token cannot do through a batch what no route lets it do, and the refusal is audited as `token.use_denied`, once a minute at most for each token, as a route's refusal of a token is (N34). A request holds at most 100 batches and a batch at most 1,000 commands, which the maintainer chose, and the pipeline's other callers are not bound by them. The check runs in the batch's transaction once the actor is authorised and holds the project's lock, so that a batch sent again while it applies waits and finds it applied, rather than failing on `applied_command`'s key: a project in the trash is still not found, and an archived one's replay is answered as it was. A batch whose commands are a recorded batch's, in the same order, is answered from the record; any other batch whose commands were all applied with `already_applied`, the latest seq of their batches, and the current revisions of the entities of those batches' operations that still exist; one that mixes them with `idempotency_mismatch`, which lists the applied ones; and one with none applied deletes the records of the refused batches that its commands were in, and is checked anew. Only a batch that a client sent (`Pipeline.Parse`) has a record, of its answer if it applies, or of its refusal if the batch and the project's state decide it before any command applies, `revision_conflict`, `limit_exceeded`, and `project_archived`, in the batch's transaction, which then commits: the batches of the resource-specific routes, as renaming the project, have new IDs that nobody sends again, so they write no record, their applied commands are recognised by `applied_command` alone, and their refusals still commit nothing; a payload is not compared with the one that its ID was applied with. The record of a batch is a row for each command, whose first holds the batch's commands and its answer once, so that a batch of many commands stores its answer once, and the others point to it and are deleted with it. An hourly job prunes the records older than 24 hours, a workspace at a time, in transactions of about 10,000 rows, each batch whole, skipping those that a batch holds, which needs the application to hold `UPDATE` of `created_at`; item 112 makes the retention configurable. Replays and mismatches are results of `storycreep_command_batches_total`, and a batch that `Pipeline.Parse` refuses counts as invalid, as one that the pipeline refuses does. Uploading needs owner, admin, or editor on the project, a row of §5.6 of its own, "Upload images (assets)" (P), and a token's `project:write`; reading needs a role on the project and `project:read`, because an asset is the draft's content. A guard refuses an upload that the role or the project would refuse whatever its file before the body is read, and the transaction checks both again; an archived project answers 409 `project_archived`, and one in the trash 404. **Not a command:** an upload adds a file that nothing refers to yet, which can be neither undone nor redone; the commands that set a node's cover or a component's portrait (items 34, 35) change the content. An asset's row never changes but for its thumbnail. **Content:** the type comes from the content alone: PNG, JPEG, GIF, and WebP by their signatures and a header that decodes, of at most 25,000,000 pixels and 16,384 on a side, and SVG by a root `svg` element of SVG's namespace in well-formed UTF-8 XML; anything else answers 422 `image_invalid`, whose `details.reason` is `type`, `malformed`, `unsupported`, or `dimensions`, and a file whose first element is `svg` but that is not well-formed XML is `malformed`, as one that names no encoding other than UTF-8 but whose bytes are not UTF-8, while one in UTF-16, with a byte order mark or without, or that declares another encoding, whatever its bytes, is well-formed XML that the server does not take, `unsupported`. An SVG is rewritten from an allowlist of SVG's elements, keeping their attributes of no namespace but event handlers and those whose values name a URL outside the file, as `ping`'s, as a browser parses one, which drops spaces only where a URL starts, so that `Layer 1: copy` names none, with references only to its own fragments or, on an image, to a `data:` URL of a raster type, and CSS without an external `url()`, an at-rule, or a backslash, and an `id` or a `class` whatever it holds, which names the element, as an editor's `icon@2x` or `Layer: 1`, so that a reference to it still finds it, and `requiredExtensions` or `requiredFeatures` whatever it holds, URLs that a browser compares with what it has and never requests, so that a `switch`'s child that names an editor's extension stays hidden, so it is never refused for what it holds, and never stored as uploaded; only the entities of its document type's internal subset whose values are text are read, and a file that has more than one document type is `malformed`, while one that is well-formed but declares another encoding, or a version of XML other than 1.0, nests its elements more than 256 deep, has an element of more than 1,024 attributes, which the equals signs of its start tag outside their quoted values count before the decoder reads them, reading past its comments, sections of CDATA, processing instructions, and document type as the decoder does, so that they, and its text and CSS, may hold any number, and any markup, or elements that enclose one another and declare more than 1,024 namespaces in all, or whose document type declares entities that are not text, or whose references to them would expand to more than 4 MiB in all, is `unsupported`, and one that, sanitized, would take more than `STORYCREEP_ASSET_MAX_BYTES`, as a character that a section of CDATA holds as it is is written as a reference, answers 413 `request_too_large`, so that a small file cannot make the server hold, or store, a huge one. An SVG's `width` and `height` are its root's, in pixels at CSS's 96 to the inch, as Inkscape's page of `210mm` is 794 pixels, or, if it says only one of them in such a unit, that one and the other in its `viewBox`'s proportions, or else its `viewBox`'s size, or none, as for one that says a size of more than 16,384 pixels on a side. **Storage:** a blob's key is `{workspace}/{sha256}.{ext}`, its thumbnail's `{workspace}/{sha256}.thumb.png`; a project has one asset per content, and the assets of a workspace with one content share its blob, which counts once towards `storage_bytes`, while thumbnails count for nothing. An upload of a blob new to the workspace checks the limit, and is refused with 413 `storage_limit_exceeded`, with `limit_exceeded`'s details; uploads that run at once may together pass it by as much as their own files, as FR-ENT-02 accepts, which the route's description and the code's say. The recount of `storage_bytes` sums the workspace's distinct blobs, so a purge's cascade drifts only until reconciliation. Until an asset's thumbnail is made, or, with the local driver, while its directory lacks a thumbnail that was made, as one not copied with the files, which the reconciliation makes again, the thumbnail route's file and 304 have `Cache-Control: private, max-age=30` instead, and with the S3 driver it redirects to the file's own route, `…/content`, with the same `Cache-Control`, rather than to a URL of the bucket signed anew, so that a browser asks for the thumbnail soon after it is made, rather than keep the large file for 5 minutes, and meanwhile reads the file through the redirect and the copy that it keeps from that route, rather than from the bucket each time.
