an asset of a content whose thumbnail the workspace has shares it, without a job; a smaller one, an SVG, one whose decoding would take more than 256 MiB of memory, as its header says, and for a JPEG every segment of the file, as a progressive JPEG whose colours are not subsampled of more than about 15 million pixels would, or a lossless WebP, or one with alpha, of more than about 9 million pixels, whose decoder holds up to 131 MiB of Huffman codes, which the upload records at once, without a job, and one that does not decode, which its job records so that an upload of the same file shares it without a job, are their own thumbnails, which the route serves until the job is done. **Orphans:** an hourly job, `asset_orphans`, deletes each blob of a workspace that no asset refers to, once the store says that it was stored at least a day before, under the blob's lock, 64 blobs in a transaction that takes their locks in one statement and holds them, whose deletions it sends the store 8 at once, so that the thousands of blobs of a purged project go within a run or a few, a workspace at a time (package `sweep`), listing a workspace's blobs from the first, in one request, if it has at most 1,000, or else from a key picked at random, past the last to the first, for half the run's budget at most, so that a run that cannot list a workspace's blobs whole reaches others than the last run did, and then reading the rows of the listed blobs alone, rather than every row of the workspace; a batch's transaction switches off `transaction_timeout`, and makes `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` 5 minutes, longer than a job runs, so that its locks hold until its deletions end, while those of a worker whose host failed without closing its connection are released within minutes, and its deletions are not cut short when the job's time ends, because a store may carry out a deletion that its client gave up on: it asks the store how old a blob is for 5 seconds at most, and sends no deletion once less than 20 seconds of the job's time are left, or once the server has begun to stop (`jobs.Stopping`), whose 20 seconds for the jobs in progress outlast the deletions that it sent, so that they end before the process exits, and waits up to 15 seconds for each that it sent, from when it sends it, and a batch that fails, as one of a blob that the store refuses to delete, leaves the next ones and the thumbnails their turn; it enqueues the thumbnail job of a content whose file the listing found and which has no thumbnail, unless one waits, 256 in a transaction, as when its job ran out of attempts while the store did not answer, and, under the lock, forgets a thumbnail that the listing reached but did not find, and that the store has not, and enqueues its job, going on past one that fails, so that the thumbnail route serves neither a large file for good nor nothing; the blobs of a deleted workspace go in item 110. An instance deletes the blobs of its workspaces that its own database does not refer to, so an instance that runs on a copy of another's database, which names the bucket that setup chose, as one to try a new version on, or to rehearse a restore (FR-DATA-05), would delete every file that the other stored after the copy was made: so each run writes the store's blob `storycreep-owner`, which names its database by the system identifier of its PostgreSQL cluster and its object ID, which a database restored from a dump, or upgraded by `pg_upgrade`, has of its own, with a token that the database records in its `instance` row, the last and the one before, and deletes nothing, warns in the log, and ends its job cancelled, or attempted again if a workspace failed too, while the blob names another database that wrote it within the week, after which it takes the store over, as an instance that replaced a lost one does, or at once if the blob is deleted. A copy of the cluster's files, as a snapshot, a point-in-time restore, or a branch of a managed database, has the same identity and tokens until one of the two claims the store again: the other then finds a token of its identity that it did not record, and neither deletes any blob until a week after either last found the other's; a copy that runs before the other claims the store again, as one started on a copy just made, whose first run comes at its start, deletes as the other does until then, which is within the hour, but none of the files that the other's users uploaded since the copy was made, which are less than a day old: an upload that finds its blob stored already, which no row of the workspace refers to, as one of a project purged an hour before, stores it again if the store says that it is more than half a day old, so that the store's time of a blob is when a row last came to refer to it, within half a day, and the reconciliation asks the store how old a blob is again just before it deletes it, as such an upload, which the copy's locks do not hold back, may have stored the blob again since the listing found it old, which leaves it only the moment between the question and the deletion. A run writes the last token again, and records none, while the store holds the one before, so that writes that the store refuses in a row, as when its disk is full, never leave it a token that the database no longer records; it writes a new token, and records it, when it finds the last, or no claim of this database's, and a blob that names no database, as one emptied rather than deleted, claims the store for none. A run reads the store's claim again before each batch of deletions, and deletes no more once another has claimed the store, as at the same moment, and reads and records the claim under an advisory lock of its own, so that two runs of one database agree, rather than a lock of the `instance` row, which first-run setup would wait for while the store answers. The owner deletes the copy's uploads, so no two instances share a bucket still, as `deploy/README.md` and the key's reference say, and the README says how to delete the blob from the `assets` volume. The reconciliation does nothing, and an upload answers 403 `setup_required` before it reads the file, until first-run setup has completed, unless `STORYCREEP_ASSET_STORAGE` names the store, because an account that `storycreep create-user` made may sign in and make projects before then, and a file that it stored in the default directory would be left behind by the bucket that setup chose. An upload's transaction that holds a blob's lock, the thumbnail job's, and the reconciliation's that asks the store again for a thumbnail, waits 30 seconds at most for the store, so that a store that does not answer fails the upload rather than hold a connection of the pool. **Configuration:** `STORYCREEP_ASSET_STORAGE` is the absolute path of a directory or an `s3://key:secret@host[:port]/bucket` URL, with `region` and `security=none`, addressed by path, and overrides the bucket that setup chose; without it, self-hosted mode's `BlobDriver` is `setup`, the bucket that setup chose or `/var/lib/storycreep/assets` (N18), and hosted mode requires an `s3://` URL. The setup API's `storage` and `STORYCREEP_SETUP_ASSET_STORAGE` record a bucket alone, in the `instance` row, because a setup screen choosing a directory of the server's would write where its user says; `GET /api/v1/setup` says `storage_fixed`. The server creates no bucket and moves no file when the store changes. **Contract:** the TypeScript generator types a body whose media types are all binary strings as a `Blob`, which the client sends as it is, and marks an operation that answers with one, whose answers the client reads as a `Blob`. **CI:** the tests of the S3 driver start `pgsty/minio`, pinned, a build of MinIO's community edition, with testcontainers, as package `dbtest` starts PostgreSQL; `STORYCREEP_TEST_MINIO_IMAGE` overrides it. Recorded in PRD 1.21 FR-ASSET-02 AC3, FR-ENT-03, FR-ADMIN-02, FR-DATA-05, §5.3, §5.6, §10.3, and ADR-009. | 29, 34, 35, 83, 110 |
| N38 | FR-PROJ-02 and §5.3 said what a board and a folder hold, but not how they change, how a folder's children are ordered, what a board's colour may be, whether a slug that a board in the trash holds is free, what deleting a folder that holds boards does, which row of §5.6 covers a folder, what a project's slug is and whether it changes, or how a command refuses a value of its payload that the project's state does not take. | Decided, with PRD 1.22; the maintainer chose that a folder is deleted only once it is empty, the named palette, and a project slug that can be changed. **Commands:** every change of a board or a folder is a command of the pipeline (N30), and no route of the API writes them but the command endpoint (item 28): `board.create`, `board.rename`, `board.describe`, `board.set_color`, `board.set_slug`, `board.move`, `board.delete`, `board.restore`, `folder.create`, `folder.rename`, `folder.move`, `folder.delete`, and `folder.restore`, each naming its entity as `id`. A create carries the new entity's ID, a UUIDv7 that the client makes, so that a client can refer to the entity before the server answers, as offline (FR-OFF-03); an ID that an entity has is refused. Each has an inverse: a create's is the deletion, to the trash, and a deletion's the restoration; a deletion of what is deleted already, and a restoration of what is not, change nothing. The reads are `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/boards`, `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/boards/{board_id}`, and `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/folders`, with an optional `kind`, which list what is not deleted, paged by ID, and take `project:read`, as reads of the draft. **Rows of §5.6:** a board's commands need "Create/rename/archive boards", and a folder's a new row, "Create, rename, move, and delete folders" (P: owner, admin, and editor), whatever the folder holds; each needs `project:write` of a token, and an archived project takes none. **Order:** the children of a folder, and those at the top, are ordered by a key of base-62 digits, `0-9A-Za-z`, that does not end in 0, of at most 128 digits, compared by its bytes, then by their IDs without the prefix, `bd_` or `fld_`, which order a board and a folder as their UUIDs do (package `orderkey`), which the client picks between its neighbours' keys, so that a move changes the moved entity alone, and the boards of a folder share its subfolders' order; §5.3's board gains `order`. A command without a key puts the entity after the parent's last child, the deleted ones included, so that one restored later keeps its place, or after its last that is not deleted once no key of at most 128 digits comes after that one, so that a key that a deleted child used up keeps nothing from being added at the end; a move without a key of the parent's last child that is not deleted into that parent keeps its key, and changes nothing, whatever deleted children come after it, which, restored, come after it again. A folder goes only into a folder of its kind, never into itself or a folder inside it, and a board only into a folder of boards; neither goes into a deleted folder. **Slugs:** package `slug` derives and checks the slugs of workspaces, projects, and boards alike. A board's is unique within its project, its trash included, so that restoring a board never changes it (AC1), and a board created without one has the first of its name's slug, `name-2`, `name-3`, and so on, that no board of the project has, with `board` for a name without letters in Latin script, and `con-board` for `con` and the other names that Windows reserves for devices, as Microsoft's file-naming rules list them, `com0` and `lpt0` among them (AC4). A typed slug that is not of the form, or is such a name, is refused with 422 `slug_invalid`, and one that another board has with 409 `slug_taken`. A project's slug is derived from its name when it is created, as a board's is, with `project` for `board`; only `project.set_slug`, the `slug` of `PATCH /api/v1/projects/{project_id}`, changes it, never a rename, and projects may share one, because they share no files. The migration derives a slug for each project that exists from its name as package `slug` does, in SQL, on a database in UTF-8, which a test compares with `slug.Derive`: it drops the nonspacing marks of Unicode's blocks of combining marks, which are those of the names in Latin, Greek, and Cyrillic script, and the variation selectors, where `slug.Derive` drops every nonspacing mark, so a mark of another script that comes between two letters in Latin script or digits splits the name there. **Colour:** `none`, or one of `red`, `orange`, `yellow`, `green`, `teal`, `blue`, `purple`, `pink`, and `gray`, which the web app maps to accessible colours. **Trash:** `board.delete` puts a board in the project's trash, and `board.restore` brings it back where it was, with the folders above it that were deleted since, whose deletion its inverse repeats; item 31 adds the trash's list, its purge, and the shared helpers of live rows. A folder is deleted to the trash too, so that the boards in the trash still name it, but is not listed there; it is deleted only once it holds no folder and no board that is not deleted, or the command is answered with 409 `folder_not_empty`. **Refusals:** a command refuses its batch for what the project's state makes of its payload, as an ID of a board that the project does not have, with `commands.Refuse`, naming the payload's field, which the API answers at that field, as `body.commands[0].payload.folder_id`, or `body.slug` for a route that dispatches the command, with 422 `validation_failed` but for the codes above. A refused batch applies nothing, and has no idempotency record (N36). **Usage:** `boards` counts the boards that are in no trash, neither their project's nor, with their project, the workspace's, approximately: creating and restoring a board add 1, deleting one takes 1, deleting a project to the workspace's trash takes its boards that are not in its own, and restoring it adds them back, counted once every command of the batch has applied (`Tx.AddUsageOnceApplied`), so that a board that a later command of the batch creates, deletes, or restores is counted with them, whatever the order of the batch's commands, and the purge of a project, whose cascade records no delta, leaves no drift, and the reconciliation recounts them. **Entry:** `project.update_settings` refuses an entry whose board is not one of the project's boards, in its trash or not, unless the entry keeps the board that it had, which may no longer exist, whatever the case of its ID's letters, so that a change of the entry's node, or the undo of a change of another setting, is not refused for it; a board in the trash is taken, so that the undo of a change of the entry from a board deleted since sets it back, and the validation reports it (FR-DEP-03's `ref_dangling`). | 30, 31 |
| N39 | FR-PROJ-06 and item 31 said that a project's trash holds what is deleted for 30 days, with a list, a restore in place, a reference-reconnection hook, and a purge, but not what the list holds and in what order, who may read it, how a client restores what it lists, what the shared helpers of live rows are, when the purge runs and what it deletes, what becomes of a deleted folder, or where its 30 days are set. | Decided, with PRD 1.23; the maintainer chose one list of every kind, the most recently deleted first, which reading project content covers, and that the reference-reconnection hook lands with its first user. **List:** `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/trash` lists the entities in the project's trash, the most recently deleted first, then by their kinds and their UUIDs, paged by a cursor of the three, each with its `kind`, `id`, `name`, `deleted_at`, and `purge_after`, when its 30 days end. `kind` takes the four kinds that FR-PROJ-06 names, `board`, `node`, `variable`, and `component`, from the start, of which only boards exist until items 33–35 add the others' tables, so that a client is never sent a kind that its schema did not list (§6.4). A deleted folder goes to the trash, so that the boards there still name it (N38), but is not listed. Listing the trash is reading project content (§5.6), and takes `project:read` of an API token. **Restore:** the command of each kind restores what the list holds where it was, as `board.restore` does (item 30); the trash has no command of its own. Nothing that refers to a board needs reconnecting when it is restored, because what refers to it, the project's entry and, from item 35, the jumpers, stays dangling while it is in the trash (N38), so the reference-reconnection hook lands in item 36 with its first user, the connections of a restored node, and the report of those that it could not reconnect (AC1). **Live rows:** each table whose rows go to the trash has a view, named `live_` and the table's name, of the table's columns over its rows that are not in the trash, which reads the table as its reader (`security_invoker`), so that row-level security holds it; the reads of the content read it, and so do, as they are added, the search (item 50), the serializer (item 76), the snapshots (item 78), and the exports (item 83) (AC2). A project in the workspace's trash is not found at all (N32), so the views need not leave out its rows. **Purge:** an hourly job, `trash_purge`, deletes for good the boards that have been in the trash for 30 days of 24 hours from their `deleted_at`, and then the deleted folders whose 30 days have passed and that hold nothing, in the trash or not, those inside others first, one project at a time, in transactions of its workspace of at most 1,000 rows, under the project's lock as a batch takes it (N30), skipping a project whose lock a batch holds, which its next run purges. It records no operation and no usage, because what is in the trash counts towards none (N38), and an undo that would restore a purged entity is answered as one of an entity that no longer exists (N30). Until the purge deletes an entity, it can be restored. The 30 days run whatever becomes of the project meanwhile: in an archived project, whose trash still lists the entity, it is restored once the project is unarchived, and in a project in the workspace's trash once the project is restored, as every write waits for those. **Registration:** a later table whose rows go to the trash adds its live view, its branch of the list if its rows are listed, and its purge, which package `trash`'s tests hold every table of a project's content with a `deleted_at` to, and a table whose rows go into a folder adds them to the folders' purge, as to their deletion (`FolderHolds`). **Retention:** the 30 days are fixed, as a project's in its workspace's trash are; item 112 makes them a setting of the workspace's retention (FR-GDPR-03), as it does the idempotency records' 24 hours (N36), so that the API never states a retention that nothing applies (N24). Recorded in PRD 1.23 FR-PROJ-06 and §5.6. | 31, 36, 112 |

| N41 | FR-VAR-01, FR-VAR-02, and item 33 said that a variable has a key unique per scope, a type, a default, a description, a folder, a scope, and `reset_on_enter`, with commands to create, update, move, and delete it, but not whether a variable in the trash keeps its key, what a default may be, which fields change once a variable exists or by which commands, how a variable is ordered among its folder's children, what becomes of a board's variables when the board goes to the trash, which row of §5.6 covers them, or which routes read them. | Decided, with PRD 1.25; the maintainer chose that a deleted variable's key is free, that a board's variables go to the trash with it, and that the key, the type, and the scope can be changed from the start. **Commands:** every change of a variable is a command of the pipeline (N30): `variable.create`, `variable.rename` (its key), `variable.describe`, `variable.set_default`, `variable.retype` (its type, with a default of the new type), `variable.set_scope` (global, or a board's), `variable.set_reset_on_enter`, `variable.move`, `variable.delete`, and `variable.restore`, each naming its variable as `id`, a create carrying a UUIDv7 that the client makes, as N38's do, and each with its inverse; a deletion of what is deleted already, and a restoration of what is not, change nothing. Item 47 has `variable.rename` rewrite the expressions that name the variable (FR-VAR-03), and refuses `variable.delete` of a used variable (FR-VAR-04), neither of which has anything to do before expressions exist. The reads are `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/variables`, paged by ID, and `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/variables/{variable_id}`, which take `project:read`, as reads of the draft, and list neither a variable in the trash nor one of a board in the trash. **Row of §5.6:** "Manage variables, components, component types" (P: owner, admin, and editor), which item 34's components and types join; each command needs `project:write` of a token, and an archived project takes none. **Keys:** an identifier of §5.8.1, `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*` of at most 64 characters, compared case by case, and none of its reserved words (package `ident`, which the expression engine's lexer, item 39, reads too), refused with 422 `key_invalid` and its rule, `format` or `reserved`, as `slug_invalid` is (AC1); unique among the project's global variables, or among a board's, of those that are not in the trash, so that a deleted variable's key is free at once, and a create, a rename, a move to another scope, or a restoration that would take a key that another variable of the scope has is refused with 409 `key_taken`. **Defaults:** a value of the type, typed by the declaration rather than by its JSON (§5.8.9): `true` or `false` for `bool`; for `int`, a number that is exactly an integer within ±(2^53 − 1), however it is written, so `3.0` and `3e0` are `3` and `4503599627370495.5` is refused; for `float`, a number read as the nearest double, which is not beyond a double's range, with `-0` written as `0`; and for `string`, a string of at most 4,096 characters, as a string literal is, without control characters but line feeds and tabs, as the other texts that people enter, and without an escaped lone surrogate. A value of another kind, as a string on an `int` (AC2), is refused with 422 `validation_failed` at the payload's `default`, and so is a number written with more than 327 characters or with an exponent beyond ±999, as `0e1000`, whatever it reads as, which no `int` or `float` needs: the shortest form of each has 24 characters at most, and `jsonb`, which holds a stored default and the operation log's inverses, whose steps an undo sends as they are read back, writes it back without an exponent, with 327 at most, as `-5e-324` is written with 324 digits after its point. Within those bounds every spelling of a number is read exactly, and the operation log, which keeps a payload as it is written, in `jsonb`, holds it, where a longer one was misread or failed its batch as the log was written. A stored default is read without the bound of its length, the database's check holding it to its type, so that a change of the bounds leaves the project's variables readable. A create or a retype without one gives the type's zero value: `false`, `0`, `0.0`, or `""`. Each is stored and shown in its canonical form, the shortest JSON that reads as it, so an integral `float` is written as an integer (§6.2). **Scope:** a board's variable names a board of the project that is not in the trash, and a global none; `reset_on_enter`, `false` by default, is set only on a board's variable, so a variable becomes global only once it is unset. **Order:** a variable sits in a folder of variables, or at the top, ordered among the folder's children by an `order` key as a board is among a folder of boards' (N38); §5.3's variable gains `order`. **Trash:** a deleted variable goes to the project's trash, listed with its key as its name, and its restoration brings it back where it was, with the deleted folders above it. A board's variables are in the trash with their board while it is there, unlisted: nothing reads them, nothing changes them, a folder that holds only them can be deleted, and they keep their keys, which nothing can take, since nothing is added to a board in the trash. Restoring the board brings them back, into their folders, which it restores with them, and its undo deletes those folders again; one of them deleted on its own, before its board, stays in the trash, listed, and is restored only once its board is, its own 30 days ending before its board's. While the board is in the trash, `variable.restore` of any of them is refused, and so is `variable.delete` of one that is not deleted on its own, since its undo, a restoration, would be refused too, as the undo of a batch that deletes the board and then the variable restores the variable first; so the undo of a variable's creation waits for its board's restoration, as the undo of its rename does. An answer of `revision_conflict` shows each as in the trash, with the board's `deleted_at` if it was not deleted on its own, as it shows any entity in the trash. A move that leaves out the key takes them for deleted children of their folder, as it takes a deleted board, since no client sees them. The purge of a board deletes its variables, by the cascade of their foreign key to it, in the board's transaction, outside the 1,000 rows that N39 holds a transaction to, so that a board is purged whole, and a restoration between two of the purge's transactions never brings it back without some of them; and the purge deletes the variables whose own 30 days have passed, among those rows, before the folders that held them. Recorded in PRD 1.25 FR-VAR-01, FR-PROJ-06, §5.3, and §5.6. | 33, 47 |
| N42 | FR-COMP-01, FR-COMP-02, and item 34 said that a project has default component types with ordered attribute definitions, a `runtime` flag, and a type change that needs an explicit conversion choice and reports what cannot convert, and that a component has a derived key, a type, an image, a rich-text description, attribute values, and a folder, but not what a `text` is, what a value may be, whether a component holds its values or reads its definitions' defaults, what becomes of the values when a default or an enum's options change, what the conversion choice is, how what cannot convert is reported, whether a type or a definition can be deleted, among which components a key is unique, how one is derived, whether a component's type changes, how components are listed and searched, which row of §5.6 covers them, or which routes read them. | Decided, with PRD 1.26; the maintainer chose a `text` of plain text, a conversion that converts or gives the new default, values that a change of the default leaves as they are, and types and definitions that go to the trash unlisted, as folders do. **Commands:** every change is a command of the pipeline (N30): `component_type.create`, `.rename`, `.set_key`, `.set_icon`, `.delete`, and `.restore`; `attribute.create`, `.rename`, `.set_key`, `.set_default`, `.set_runtime`, `.set_options`, `.retype`, `.move`, `.delete`, and `.restore`; and `component.create`, `.rename`, `.set_key`, `.describe`, `.set_image`, `.set_attribute`, `.move`, `.delete`, and `.restore`, each naming its entity as `id`, a create carrying a UUIDv7 that the client makes, as N38's do, and each with its inverse; a deletion of what is deleted already, and a restoration of what is not, change nothing. Item 47 has `component.set_key` rewrite the expressions that name the component (FR-COMP-02 AC2) and refuses `component.delete` of a used component (FR-COMP-04). **Reads:** `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/component-types` and `/component-types/{component_type_id}`, each type with its definitions that are not in the trash, in their order; `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/components`, which lists components without their descriptions and values, so that a page stays small, filtered by `component_type_id` and by `query`, of at most 100 characters, that their names or keys hold, compared without regard to case (AC3); `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/components/{component_id}`, with the description and the values by their definitions' IDs; and `GET .../component-types/{component_type_id}/attributes/{attribute_id}/conversion?type=…&options=…`, the components, those in the trash included, whose values would not convert, the definition and its values read as of one moment, a page at a time in one snapshot of the database, which locks nothing, so that what a change commits meanwhile is not read, with an enum's options one to a line, which no option holds a line feed for, and a target that the retype refuses answered as it is; each is paged by ID and takes `project:read`. **Row of §5.6:** "Manage variables, components, component types" (P: owner, admin, and editor), which N41 foresaw; each command needs `project:write` of a token, and an archived project takes none. **Types:** a project is created with Character, Item, Location, Faction, and Custom, keys `character` to `custom`, without definitions, named from the server's catalog in the project's default locale, in the transaction that creates it (`project.Options.Seed`); the migration gives the projects that exist them in English. A type's key is an identifier unique among the project's types that are not in the trash, and its icon one of `none`, `person`, `group`, `item`, `location`, `event`, `book`, `star`, `heart`, `key`, `gem`, `shield`, `flag`, and `tag`. **Definitions:** a key unique among the type's definitions that are not in the trash (AC3), a name, a type, an enum's options, a default, `runtime`, and an `order` among the type's definitions, a key of package `orderkey`, as a board has among its folder's children (N38); at most 100 that are not in the trash per type. A value is typed as a variable's default is (N41), in package `value`, which item 33's defaults move to: a `string` of at most 4,096 characters, a `text` of at most 100,000, which is never `runtime`, and an `enum` one of 1 to 100 options of 1 to 100 characters, no two alike, none holding U+FFFD, which an escape of a lone surrogate is read as, so that the server holds the options that the client sent; without a default, the type's zero value, an enum's first option. **Values:** each component holds one of each of its type's definitions, a row of `attribute_value` with an ID of its own, a UUIDv7 that the database makes, and neither `rev` nor `deleted_at`: a change of a value is a change of its component, whose `rev` it increments, so that adding, deleting, or restoring a definition, or a change of its options or type that changes values, increments each component's whose values it changes. Adding a definition gives each component of the type, those in the trash included, its default, in one command (AC1); `attribute.set_default` gives the new default only to the components created later. `attribute.set_options` gives a value that is none of the new options the default, unless its `values` give the component another; a default that is none of them is refused. **Retype:** `attribute.retype` takes `conversion`, `convert` or `reset` (AC2): `convert` converts each value as the expression language's `to_int`, `to_float`, and `to_string` do (§5.8.4, §5.8.9), a string to a `float` however many digits it has and however large its exponent, a `bool` as 0 or 1, a number that is 0 or 1, and a string that is `true` or `false`, as a `bool`, a string within a `string`'s length as a `text` or a `string`, with its characters as they are, as a stored value is read, and a value whose string is an option as that option, and gives the new default to each that does not convert; `reset` gives every value the new default; `values` give components values of their own, and pass over a component that the project no longer has, as one that the purge has deleted, so that an undo applies once the purge has deleted a component whose value it gives back, as `set_options`'s do; one of another type is refused. A retype to the definition's own type is refused, so an enum's options change with `set_options`, and so is one of a `runtime` definition to a `text`. A float is written as `to_string` writes it, positional from 1e-6 to below 1e21 with one digit after the point when integral, and with an exponent otherwise, as JavaScript writes it (`semantics.FormatFloat`, item 38). The undo of a retype or of `set_options` holds only the values that converting back does not give back. The pipeline bounds the undo of every batch, whatever its commands: it refuses, with 422 `validation_failed`, the command whose inverse would take the payloads of the steps of its batch's inverse, as JSON encodes them for the operation log, escapes included, past 4 MiB, so that the batch that undoes it, sent with each command's ID and expected revisions (item 37), fits in a request's body of 5 MiB. It refuses a retype or `set_options` at the field that it changes, its `type` or `options` (`commands.Spec.UndoField`), and any other command, as a description's, at its payload; a retype or `set_options` stops reading the values that it loses once they pass what the bound leaves of the batch's undo (`commands.Tx.UndoRoom`). In place of a retype refused so, deleting the definition and adding another costs the undo nothing. The undo is a batch as any other, bounded alike by its own inverse, which writes again what the undone batch's commands wrote, so the undo of a batch whose commands wrote more than 4 MiB, as several large descriptions over small ones, is refused too, and is sent in smaller batches; item 37 decides how a client undoes one so. The mebibyte that the bound leaves of the body holds the expected revisions of some ten thousand entities. The undo of `set_options` gives a value that is none of the old options the old default, and that of a retype one that does not convert back, so neither holds a value that the undo gives back so. **Components:** a key unique among the project's components that are not in the trash (AC1), so that a deleted component's key is free and its restore is refused while another has it, and derived from the name unless the command gives one: its letters and digits in lowercase ASCII, without accents, as a slug is (package `slug`), each run of other characters an underscore, with `_` before a leading digit, or `component` for a name without them, and then `_2`, `_3`, and so on while another has it or it is a reserved word, as a type's and a definition's are, with `type` and `attribute` for a name without them. A rename leaves the key (AC2). The type is fixed once the component is created; the image is an asset of the project, which a foreign key holds; the description is a document of package `richtext`, stored in its canonical form; and the component sits in a folder of components with an `order`, as a variable does. **Trash:** a deleted component goes to the project's trash, listed with its name; its restore restores the folders above it, and its type, if it was deleted since, whose key must then be free. A type is deleted, to the trash, unlisted, only once each of its components is in the trash, or the command is answered with 409 `component_type_in_use`; its definitions are in the trash with it, as a board's variables are with their board (N41). A definition is deleted to the trash, unlisted, keeping its components' values, which its restore brings back, giving its default to a component created meanwhile. The purge deletes a component with its values, a definition with its values, and a type once it holds no component, in the trash or not, with its definitions, each once its 30 days have passed (N39). **Refusals:** a key that is not an identifier is answered with 422 `key_invalid`, one that another entity of its scope has with 409 `key_taken`, as for a variable, and a document that `richtext.Parse` refuses at the payload's field joined with its location in the document, as `payload.description.content[0]`, with the error's message, which holds its pointer (N40). Recorded in PRD 1.26 FR-COMP-01, FR-COMP-02, FR-PROJ-06, §5.3, and §5.6. | 34, 39, 47 |
| N43 | FR-EDIT-02, FR-EDIT-05, FR-EDIT-06, and item 35 said that a node is an element, a branch, or a jumper with a kind, a title, a text, a speaker, a cover, a colour, tags, a position kept to 2 decimal places, a size, and `is_ending`, changed by a create, an update, a batched move, a deletion, and a duplicate, and that deleting a board lists the jumpers that target it and needs confirmation, but not whether a node's type changes, what a title, a position, or a size may be, which fields each type has, what a speaker or a jumper's target may be, what becomes of a target that is deleted, whether tags that differ in case are one, how a deletion is confirmed, what becomes of a board's nodes when it goes to the trash, which row of §5.6 covers a node, or which routes read nodes. | Decided, with PRD 1.27; the maintainer chose that a board's deletion is confirmed by a flag of its command, that tags are compared without regard to case, that a node may have no size, and one command that changes the fields that it names. **Commands:** every change of a node but the purge of its speaker (below) is a command of the pipeline (N30): `node.create`, `node.update`, `node.move`, `node.duplicate`, `node.delete`, and `node.restore`, each naming its node as `id`, a create and a duplicate carrying a UUIDv7 that the client makes, as N38's do, and each with its inverse; a deletion of what is deleted already, and a restoration of what is not, change nothing. `node.update` changes the fields that its payload names and leaves the others, and its undo sets back exactly those: the empty string removes a speaker or a cover, `{}` a size or a target, and `[]` the tags. `node.move` moves a selection of up to 10,000 nodes of any of the project's boards, each named once, as one command, changing their positions alone, and passes over a node that is where it goes already (FR-EDIT-02 AC2). `node.duplicate` copies a node that is not in the trash onto its board at another position, with every field but its position. `board.set_entry_node` sets or removes a board's entry node. **Rows of §5.6:** a node's commands need "Edit nodes, connections, text, expressions" (P: owner, admin, and editor), a new operation of the matrix, which item 36's connections join; `board.set_entry_node` needs "Create/rename/archive boards", as a board's other changes do; each needs `project:write` of a token, and an archived project takes none. **Reads:** `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/boards/{board_id}/nodes`, a board's nodes whole, an element with its text; `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/nodes/{node_id}`; `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/nodes/{node_id}/referenced-by`, the jumpers that target a node, on every board (FR-EDIT-05 AC2); and `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/boards/{board_id}/referenced-by`, the jumpers on the project's other boards that target the board's nodes, each of them paged by ID, without what is in the trash; and `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/tags?prefix=…`, at most 100 of the project's tags that start with a prefix of at most 50 characters, compared without regard to case, each once, in the spelling that most of its nodes use, with how many nodes have it, the most used first. Each takes `project:read`. **Fields:** the type is fixed once the node is created; only an element has a kind, `dialogue` by default, a text, an empty paragraph by default, stored in its canonical form (N40), a speaker, a cover, and `is_ending`, and only a jumper a target, and a command that gives one of them to another type is refused at the field with 422 `validation_failed`; a title has at most 200 characters, and is empty, or a name as `input.CheckTrimmedName` takes one; the colour is of the boards' palette (N38). **Numbers:** a position's x and y are numbers from −1,000,000,000 to 1,000,000,000, and a size's width and height from 1 to 100,000, each read as the decimal that it is written as, not as a double, and rounded half away from zero to 2 decimal places, so that `0.125` is `0.13`, and `240`, `240.0`, and `2.4e2` are one position, written back without trailing zeros, as `240` (FR-EDIT-02 AC4, §6.2), and held by `numeric(12,2)`; a number written with more than 327 characters or with an exponent beyond ±999 is refused, as a value's is (N41). A size is both a width and a height, or neither, as `{}`, for which the canvas lays the node out by itself. **Tags:** at most 32 of a node, each of 1 to 50 characters, without white space around it and without control characters, kept as written and compared in Unicode's normalization form C with their case folded, so that a node holds a tag once, whatever its case. **References:** a speaker is a component of the project, of any type, a jumper's target a node of the project and that node's board, and a board's entry node a node of the board, each in the trash or not, so that the undo of a change from one deleted since sets it back, or the one that its entity keeps, which may no longer exist; one that the purge has deleted is not taken otherwise, so that an undo that would set it back is refused, as N30 refuses one that would restore a purged entity. A cover is an asset of the project. A speaker and a cover have foreign keys: the purge of a component leaves each element whose speaker it is, in the trash or not, without one, and increments the element's revision, as every change of a node does (§5.7), though no command makes the change; an asset is deleted only with its project. Neither a target nor an entry has a foreign key, so that a deleted or purged node stays named, dangling, for the validation to report (FR-EDIT-05 AC1, FR-DEP-03), as it reports a jumper without a target, or one that targets a jumper (`jumper_broken`, `jumper_invalid`). The project's entry node (N32) is a node of its entry board, unless the entry keeps both its board and its node. **Board deletion:** `board.delete` of a board whose nodes, not in the trash, jumpers on the project's other boards, not in the trash either, target is refused with 409 `board_referenced`, whose details list the first 100 of them, by ID, each with its `id`, `board_id`, and `title`, and `jumper_count`, how many there are, unless its payload sets `confirm`, which leaves them with their targets (FR-PROJ-02 AC2); a jumper whose target is deleted already, or purged, dangles already, and is not among them. The undo of a board's restoration confirms the deletion again, as the deletion that put the board in the trash had to be confirmed if it was; the undo of a board's creation does not. **Trash:** a deleted node goes to the trash, listed by its title, which may be empty; a board's nodes are in the trash with it, as its variables are (N41): unlisted, nothing changes them, not even a deletion, but the purge of a speaker, as above, they come back with the board, and its purge deletes them by cascade; one deleted on its own, before its board, is listed, restored only once its board is, and purged once its own 30 days have passed. **Usage:** `nodes` counts the nodes that are in no trash, neither their project's, themselves or with their board, nor, with their project, the workspace's, approximately: creating, duplicating, and restoring a node add 1, deleting one takes 1, deleting a board takes its nodes that are not deleted themselves, restoring it adds them back, and deleting a project to the workspace's trash takes its nodes, and restoring it adds them back, counted once every command of the batch has applied, as its boards are (N38); the reconciliation recounts them (N31). In FR-ENT-02 AC3's test, the 16 sessions wait for their projects' locks, but never for a lock of the usage. Recorded in PRD 1.27 FR-EDIT-02, FR-EDIT-05, FR-EDIT-06, FR-PROJ-02, FR-PROJ-06, §5.3, and §5.6. | 35, 36, 48 |
| N44 | FR-EDIT-03, FR-EDIT-02 AC3, FR-PROJ-06 AC1, and item 36 said that a connection leaves a node's output port for another node, carries a rich-text label, can be reconnected at either end, deleted, and reordered, changing only its `order`, that deleting a node deletes its connections in the same transaction and its undo restores both, and that restoring a node reconnects its connections where both endpoints exist and reports which could not be restored, but not which nodes a connection may leave or enter, what its order is, what a connection without a label has, which commands change it, what becomes of one deleted on its own, how a node's deletion takes its connections, whether one comes back once the node at its other end is restored later, where the report goes, which row of §5.6 covers a connection, or which routes read connections. | Decided, with PRD 1.28; the maintainer chose that a node's connections are in the trash with it, that the report of what a restoration could not reconnect is in the batch's answer, and that a jumper, and a branch until its outputs, has no connections out. **Ends:** a connection is of a board, the board of both of its nodes, which is fixed: it leaves an element of the board by the port `out`, and enters a node of the board of any type, the element itself among them, by as many connections as are drawn, each a node of the project that is not in its trash; a node of another board is refused at its field with 422 `validation_failed`, and so are a jumper, which has no connections out (§5.8.5), and a branch, whose connections leave its outputs, which item 43 adds, with a branch output's ID as `from_port`, which is `out` until then. **Order:** the connections that leave a port are ordered by a key of package orderkey, then by their IDs, as a folder's children are (N38), so that reordering one changes its key alone (FR-EDIT-03 AC2); one created, or reconnected to leave another node, without a place goes after the node's last, those in the trash included. **Label:** a rich-text document in its canonical form (N40), an empty paragraph for none. **Commands:** `connection.create`, `connection.update` (the fields that it names: the label, which item 43's condition and on-traverse assignments join), `connection.reconnect` (either end, or both, to nodes of its board), `connection.reorder`, `connection.delete`, and `connection.restore`, each naming its connection as `id`, a create carrying a UUIDv7 that the client makes, as N38's do, and each with its inverse; they need "Edit nodes, connections, text, expressions" (P), as a node's do (N43), and `project:write` of a token, and an archived project takes none. **Reads:** `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/boards/{board_id}/connections`, a board's connections whole, with their labels, paged by ID, and `GET /api/v1/projects/{project_id}/connections/{connection_id}`, each without what is in the trash, and each taking `project:read`. **Trash:** a connection deleted on its own goes to the trash, unlisted, as a folder does (N39), so that the undo of its deletion restores it, and is purged once its own 30 days have passed. A node's connections are in the trash with it, and with its board, without a change of their own, as a board's nodes are (N43): nothing changes them meanwhile, not even a deletion, and one comes back once neither of its nodes is in the trash, whichever is restored last, while the purge of either node deletes it, by cascade. So deleting a node deletes its connections in its own command, and its undo, the node's restoration, restores both (FR-EDIT-02 AC3). **Reconnection hook:** a command that restores an entity from the trash that connections refer to reports it to the pipeline (`Tx.Restored`), as `node.restore` does a node, and `board.restore` a board, whose nodes come back with it, and once every command of the batch has applied, the pipeline asks each kind that refers to the restored kind, registered as a reconnection (`commands.RegisterReconnection`), which of its entities are still not reconnected; the batch's answer lists them as `not_reconnected`, each once, with the IDs of the entities in the trash that it waits for as `waiting_for`, so that a batch that restores both of a connection's nodes lists nothing (FR-PROJ-06 AC1). A board's restoration lists the connections of the nodes that come back with it whose node at the other end is deleted on its own, and not one neither of whose nodes comes back. The batch's idempotency record keeps the list with its answer, and an answer of `already_applied` has none. A connection whose other node the purge has deleted is gone with it, and is not listed. Recorded in PRD 1.28 FR-EDIT-02, FR-EDIT-03, FR-PROJ-06, FR-API-03, §5.3, and §5.6. | 36, 43, 48 |
| N45 | FR-EDIT-08 and item 37 said that undo and redo are sent as inverse commands with expected revisions, refused with an explanation and no partial change when they would overwrite newer work, and that a property test applies N random commands, undoes N, and finds the state equal to the original, but not how a client gets the inverse of a batch that it sent, which the operation log kept and nothing handed out, which revisions an undo expects, how a user's consecutive undos of one entity pass the check of the revisions that the first of them moves, what a refused undo answers, or what the equality makes of an entity that a command created, whose undo sends it to the trash. | Decided, with PRD 1.29; the maintainer chose that the undo is in the batch's answer, that it expects the revisions that the batch left and the client moves them after its own undo, that a refused undo answers `revision_conflict` as any stale batch does, and that the property test compares what is outside the trash and what was in it. **Answer:** a batch's result holds `undo`, its inverse as the operation log keeps it, the commands' inverses from the last command's to the first's, each `{"type", "payload", "expected_rev"}`, which the client sends back as a batch, each command with a new `command_id`; the answer to that batch holds its own undo, which redoes the batch. `undo` is `null` for a batch with a command that cannot be undone, for one `already_applied`, whose commands' batches each have one of their own, and for one answered again once compaction has dropped its operation (FR-GDPR-03), and `[]` for one whose inverses have no steps, as the unarchiving of a project that is not archived, or whose commands changed nothing; a replay of a batch while its idempotency record is kept reads the undo again from the operation log, as its answer held it, byte for byte. **Revisions:** each command of an undo expects the revisions that the batch left of the entities that the command that it undoes changed (`Tx.Changed`), each given to every step of that command's inverse whose type touches the entity's kind; an entity of a kind that none of its steps touches is one that the undo does not write, and none of them expects it. An entity that several of the batch's commands changed is expected by the undo of each of them, and one that a command whose inverse has several steps of its kind changed by each of them, as each deletion of the folders that a restore brought back expects all of them: the whole undo is checked against the entities as they are before it, where once would do, but an undo sent in smaller batches (**Bounds**) is checked part by part, the client may split it anywhere, as nothing in it says where one command's steps end, and a part that did not expect an entity that a part after it writes would let that part write it back over another user's change since. The bound counts each, so a batch of many changes of a type's attribute definitions, each of which changes every component of the type, holds each component's revision once per change, and on a type of many components is refused, and sent in smaller batches, each an undo step of its own; and so is a restore that brings back some 280 folders from the trash, whose undo's deletions each expect every folder, which applies once those folders are restored first, from the top down. A command that changed nothing, which reported no change, has nothing to undo, and the undo holds none of its inverse's steps, which would expect no revision, and so write back what the command found over whatever another user changed since, as the undo of a rename of a board to the name that it has would rename it back over another user's rename; the bounds give back the room that they took, and are held as each command reports its changes (`Tx.Changed`), which such a command does not, so that a batch is not refused for such a command's steps, wherever it is in the batch. The undo is checked as any batch is, against the entities as they are before it, so it is refused whole, with 409 `revision_conflict` and every stale entity's current state, once any of them has changed since, by anyone. A client's own undo brings each entity that the batch changed back as it was before the batch, at a new revision, so the client moves what the other entries of its undo and redo stacks expect of the entity, from the revision that it had before the undone batch, which the client's store holds once it has every operation of the project before the batch's seq, to the one that the undo's answer gives, as the property test's client, the project's only writer, does; a store that had not yet heard of another user's batch that came between when the answer came catches up first (PRD §10.6: it reconciles with the server's operations by seq), so that it does not take that user's change for the batch's and move an entry that expects the entity as it was before it; an entry that expects another revision of it, as one that another user's change came between, is left as it is, and is refused. An undo may still change what the batch did not change, whose revisions it does not expect, as its commands would at any time: the undo of a creation, a deletion, takes to the trash with what it deletes what has been put in it since, as the nodes that another user added to a board that the batch created (`board.delete`); the undo of a deletion, a restore, brings back the folders that what it restores is in, and a component's type, which may have gone to the trash since (`board.restore`, `folder.restore`, `variable.restore`, `component.restore`); and the undo of `attribute.set_options` or `attribute.retype` gives the default to a value that a component was given since, of an option or a type that the undo takes away, as those commands do to any value that they cannot keep. The undo's own undo sets each of them back. FR-EDIT-08 refuses the undo of a command whose target changed since, which the revisions do; refusing such an undo too would need a mode of those commands that refuses what the batch did not change, left to the maintainer. **Bounds:** the bound of a batch's undo, 4 MiB, counts the revisions that its commands expect, each as its entity's ID and 19 digits, as a command reports its changes, so that one that changes more than its undo could expect stops there, besides the payloads of its steps, each number in them as the log's jsonb writes it, without an exponent, as the answer holds it (`commands.LogBytes`), and the steps of an undo are at most 1,000, the commands of a batch, so that the batch that undoes another fits in one request; a batch that would pass either is refused at the command that takes it past, with 422 `validation_failed` whose `details.reason` is `undo_too_large`, the one reason that `validation_failed` gives, so that a client tells an undo refused so from one refused for what it writes, which smaller batches do not mend. The batch that undoes another is bounded alike, by its own undo, which writes again what the other's commands wrote, so an undo may be refused so; N42 left to this item how a client then undoes the batch: it sends the undo in smaller batches, split anywhere, in its order, each once the one before it has applied, with what it expects of an entity that those before it brought back moved from the revision that the batch left, which the undo expects, to the one that the answer of the last of them that changed the entity gives, and not from the revision that the entity had before the batch, which the undo does not expect, as it moves its stacks (`TestUndoInParts` and `TestUndoOfRestoreInParts` move their parts so), so that each is refused, applying nothing, if what it writes back has changed since, though those before it stay applied, and their answers hold the undos that redo them. **Archival and deletion:** the pipeline checks a batch against the project's archived state and its trash before any of its commands applies, so the undo of a batch that changes the content and archives or deletes the project, which unarchives or restores it and then sets the content back, would be refused as a batch of an archived project, or of one in the trash. A batch with a command that unarchives the project, or restores it (`commands.Spec.Unarchives`, `Spec.Restores`), is therefore not refused for the archive, or for the trash, whatever its other commands, as a batch of its own that unarchived or restored the project first would not be; a project both archived and in the trash is archived still once restored, so a batch that restores it applies other commands only if it unarchives it too, or they apply to an archived project. The actor needs the role that unarchiving or restoring needs anyway. Unarchiving a project in the trash, which only such a batch that restores it does, declares the project that the pair adds to the workspace's projects, which the restoration of an archived project does not; and such a batch of a project in the trash adds to the workspace's boards and nodes what the restoration counts once the batch has applied, which the changes of its other commands, before the restoration or after, are part of, so the pipeline drops their own deltas (`Tx.AddUsageOnceApplied`). **Property test:** for each of three seeds, random batches of every type of command that has an inverse, through the command endpoint, over a project with entities of each kind, some of them in its trash; then every batch undone, last first, and every row of the project's content equal to what it was, its revisions, timestamps, and authors apart, but for the rows that the batches created, which may be in the trash; then every batch redone, and the content equal to what the batches left. A seed makes the same batches whenever it runs, the IDs of what they create included, so a seed that fails fails again. A type with an inverse that the test has no generator for fails it, so a new type joins it; a type without one is listed in the test with why it has none. Recorded in PRD 1.29 FR-EDIT-08, FR-API-03, and §5.3. | 37, 56 |
| N46 | §5.8.9 and item 38 said that the Go, TypeScript, and GDScript evaluators case and trim strings by the tables of one pinned version of Unicode, generated from the Unicode Character Database into `/schema/unicode/`, and that numbers follow rules of their own, with cases in `/schema/expr-corpus/`, but not which version, what in the database the tables come from or where its files are kept, how a `float` with an exponent is written, what a format specifier rounds, the double or the decimal that `to_string` writes, or how it writes a large value, a value that rounds to zero, or an `int`, whether an implementation may fuse two `float` operations, whether a `float` divided by zero is `division_by_zero` or `float_not_finite`, whether an underflow is an error, or how the corpus writes a case before the parser of item 39 exists. | Decided, with PRD 1.30; the maintainer chose Unicode 18.0.0, the database's files kept in the repository, and the rounding of the decimal that `to_string` writes: the tables are of Unicode 18.0.0, generated from the simple mappings of `UnicodeData.txt` and the `White_Space` of `PropList.txt`, which `/schema/unicode/ucd/` holds with Unicode's licence and with their SHA-256 checked, into `/schema/unicode/tables.json` and the Go package's copy, which CI regenerates offline on every run; a `float` with an exponent is written as JavaScript's `String` writes it; a format specifier rounds the decimal that `to_string` writes half away from zero and writes it in positional notation, without a sign when it rounds to zero, an `int` as its widened `float`; each `float` operation rounds once and none is fused; a division by `0.0` is `division_by_zero`; and an underflow is `0.0` or a subnormal, without an error. `server/internal/semantics` holds the Go primitives, which package `value`'s conversions use too. | N47 | §5.8.1, §5.8.2, §5.8.8, and item 39 said that a lexer and a parser read the grammar's sketch, word operators, script terminators, comments, and `node("nd_…")` literals among it, into a deterministic AST of nine node kinds, with errors of stable codes and exact character ranges and a corpus of cases, but not what a number of neither literal's form is, whether a float literal beyond a double's range is an error, which characters are white space, which line breaks end a statement, what a `\uXXXX` may name, how a range is counted, which error a source of several gives, how terminators repeat, which forms a qualified name has, how deeply parentheses nest, when an AST's node count is checked, the fields of each node kind and the names of its operators, what the AST keeps of how its source is written, how a float nearer 0 than 1e-6 or a string's characters are written, how the parser names what name resolution (item 40) has not resolved, or the format of the corpus's cases. | Decided, with PRD 1.31. **Lexing:** white space is a space, a tab, a CR, or an LF; in a script, a line break outside parentheses after an identifier, a literal, or `)`, though not after `global` or `board`, ends the statement before it, and every other line break, and every line break of a condition, is white space, so that a statement goes on after an operator, a comma, or an opening parenthesis; an integer literal is `[0-9]+`, leading zeros and all, and a float literal `[0-9]+\.[0-9]+([eE][+-]?[0-9]+)?`, read as `to_float` reads one, and `float_out_of_range` beyond a double's range, and any other run of ASCII letters, digits, `_`, and `.` that starts with a digit is `invalid_number`, a letter that is not ASCII ending the run, as any character that starts no token does; and a character that is not ASCII outside a string or a comment, or a NUL or a byte that is not UTF-8 anywhere, right after a backslash too, is `invalid_character`, though one in place of a `\u`'s digits ends them, so that the `\u` is `invalid_escape` first. **Errors:** each has one of the 18 codes that `/schema/expr-corpus/README.md` lists, with the range of each, and the range of characters that it covers, counted in code points from 0, which an editor converts to its own units; the parser stops at the first error that it meets from left to right, reading no token ahead of the one that it is at but the one after a node reference's string, so that a token that it has not read is never the error, a token that cannot be read where the parser stops at it, though not where the parser reads it only to tell that it is not a dot, as after `global` or a name's last part, or not the `)` after a node reference's string, nor after a node reference's `)`, so that the error of what the tokens before it make comes first there, as it does before any other token, a statement read as an expression first, so that one that is neither an assignment nor a call is `invalid_statement` over all of it once what ends it follows it, and any other token after it is that token's error, and an assignment's operator in a condition, wherever it is, `condition_must_be_pure` at the operator, which meets FR-EXPR-02 AC3 in item 39 rather than in item 40. **Grammar:** terminators repeat, before, between, and after statements; The parser counts an AST's nodes, the node count of §5.8.6, as it reads the tokens that make them, each as soon as it reaches it: a literal, but for a node reference's string; the identifier or the qualifier that a name, a call, or a node reference starts with; a unary or a binary operator; and a statement's assignment operator, after a script's own node. The token that makes the 513th is `ast_too_large`, so that no source costs more than an AST of 512 nodes, where one of 2 MiB made an AST of some 240 MiB before any limit refused it, and so that an AST that the parser returns is at most 512 deep, which a walk of it may recurse once per level of. A token that cannot be read, which the lexer reads whole before the parser reaches it, makes no node, nor do `null`, `if`, `else`, and `elseif`, so that each is its own error where the 513th node would be, while a name or a qualifier counts at its first word, before its parts or its dot are read; cases of the corpus pin both. so an expression is always what compiling its source against the project as it is gives, whatever batches led there, the undo property test's round trip gives back what a batch found (FR-EDIT-08 AC2), and expressions in the trash are compiled as the others are, current when restored. An expression of a board in the trash is compiled as it would be once the board is restored, against what the project's expressions can name and the board's own variables and nodes that are in the trash with it alone (`expr.Project.With`), which no other board's expression names, as one of another board in the trash does not either, so that a board's deletion and its restoration change none of the expressions that it holds, nor their owners, and compile none of them, and the near matches of another board's expression, which name its own board's variables and the global ones alone, none either. A valid expression's AST is not kept against its source: a rename, or a new variable that shadows a name, changes what its source compiles to, and until item 47 rewrites the sources that a rename or a shadow changes, so that they compile to the ASTs that they named, a source that names a renamed key no longer compiles. **Owners:** an expression is part of its owner, so a change of it that the API shows, its source's or its compilation's, increments its owner's revision, once per batch, which the batch's answer lists, but for an owner deleted to the trash itself, which nothing changes until its restoration gives it a new revision, so that the undo of its deletion still applies, while one that is there only with its board, its node, or its output, whose container's restoration gives it none, is changed as a live one is; the derivation's changes are reported as a command's are (`Tx.Changed`), but no step of the batch's undo expects them, since the undo's own batch compiles them again. | N52 | NFR-Q-06, §9.1, and item 45 said that a seeded generator produces the `small`, `medium`, `large`, and `dense` fixtures deterministically through commands, of the counts that §9.1 gives, and that a Go benchmark suite runs in CI on fixed hardware and fails on a server regression of more than 20%, but not what a fixture holds beyond those counts, as the variables of `small` and `dense`, components, branches, jumpers, connections, expressions, and texts, what "deterministically" covers, how a fixture is loaded for manual testing, what fixed hardware GitHub's runners, whose machines vary from job to job, give, what a regression is measured on and against, or how a benchmark checks its target. | Decided, with PRD 1.38; the maintainer chose that CI measures a change and its base on the same runner, rather than on a runner of the project's own, and that a fixture is loaded for manual testing through the API, with an API token. **Generator:** `server/internal/fixture` makes a fixture (`Generate`) of its size and a seed alone: the batches of commands that build it, each of at most 500 commands (`MaxBatch`), every ID of which, an entity's and a command's, is a UUIDv7 whose milliseconds count up from 2026-01-01T00:00:00Z and whose random bits come from the seed's own source, so that the same size and seed give the same batches, byte for byte, on every platform, and the same project, but for what the server makes of its own, the IDs of its expressions and of its default component types, and its rows' times and authors. An element has one choice, the next node, or, one time in three, two or three, each labelled, some with a condition, to nodes of the next few; a jumper's target, which the last batch sets once every node exists, is the next board's entry node six times in ten, and otherwise an earlier node of its own board. A condition reads a variable that its board reads, a runtime attribute, or whether a node that is no jumper was visited; a script assigns variables and runtime attributes, or signals; and two in ten sentences of a text show a value, of a variable or a runtime attribute, and some mention a component. Every expression compiles, and no command names what an earlier one has not created. `go test ./internal/fixture` checks that a seed gives the same batches, by a digest of each size's, which a change of the generator updates on purpose, since every benchmark's project changes with it; that every size's batches are well formed; and that `small`, loaded into two databases, gives the same project in both, and `medium` and `dense` load, every expression of them compiling. `large`, which takes a minute or more to load, is loaded by the benchmark job, on every pull request. **Manual testing:** `go run ./cmd/fixturegen -size medium -server … -project prj_… -token-file …` sends a fixture's batches to the command endpoint of a new project, one a request, with an API token of `project:write` (`fixture.Sender`), since only a session creates a project (FR-AUTH-04); it waits out a 429 and sends a batch that failed again, and, run again, resumes where it stopped. With `-out`, it writes the requests' bodies instead. **Benchmarks:** a server metric of §9.1 is a Go benchmark of a project of a fixture, which its owner, signed in, edits through the API, as the web app does, on a PostgreSQL server that keeps what it commits, as a production server does, which `dbtest` starts for a test binary's benchmarks, apart from its tests' server, so that a benchmark times what a commit costs, and whose database is vacuumed, analyzed, and checkpointed once the fixture has loaded, so that no autovacuum or checkpoint that the load leaves to come lands in the timed batches of some runs and not of others. NFR-PERF-07's is `BenchmarkCommandRoundTrip`, in `server/internal/httpserver`, on `large`: each of its sub-benchmarks is a batch that an author sends most, the change of a text, of a script, and of a position, a node created and connected, the change of a condition, and of a variable's default, which reports its p95 (`p95-ms`) and fails over the target, 150 ms; each batch sets a value that no other batch of the benchmark sets, so that it changes what it names, rather than being the no-op that the server makes of a batch that changes nothing. CI's benchmark job checks out the base, the first parent of the merge commit that a pull request is checked out as, or the commit that a push replaced, or the commit before the push's where the checkout has none, as after a force-push, builds the tests of both, outside the runs' bounds, and runs `go test -run '^$' -bench . -benchtime 300x -count 1 -p 1` of the packages whose tests have a benchmark, as `go test -list` names them, of the base and of the change, in turn, four times each, on one runner, in the order base, change, change, base, twice, so that both are measured on the same hardware and a steady drift of the machine during the job falls on both alike. Each run is a go test of its own, with a database server and a fixture of its own, and is one sample of each metric of its package's benchmarks, the median of its values if it ran a benchmark more than once, since what makes a run fast or slow, as the state that the load left the database in, is the same for each of its counts. A run has at most ten minutes, however many packages it benchmarks, and the job's time holds eight runs at that bound; the base is not run again once a run of it outlasts them, and a base whose benchmarks fail otherwise is compared as far as it ran. `cmd/benchgate` then compares each metric whose unit is a time, `ns/op` or one that ends in `-ms` or `-ns`, of each package's benchmarks, and fails the build where the change's median is more than 20% above the base's and a Mann-Whitney U test of the runs finds the two apart at p < 0.05, which four runs of each side reach only where every run of the change is slower than every run of the base, so that a shared runner's noise seldom fails the build; a benchmark that only one side has is not compared, nor is a time metric of too few runs on a side for the test to reach p < 0.05, as of a base that failed in some of its runs, which the report's first line counts, and a GitHub annotation names each time metric of the base that the change does not have, as one of a benchmark that it renamed, each of the change that the base does not have, as one of a benchmark that it adds, or one that failed in every run of the base, and each of too few runs.
